                Electronic Colloquium on Computational Complexity, Revision 2 of Report No. 179 (2026)




      On the Hardness of 4-to-1 Games with Perfect Completeness
                         Yumou Fei∗                Dor Minzer†               Shuo Wang‡


                                                     Abstract
         We prove that for all ε > 0, there exists a positive integer k such that given a 4-to-1 game Ψ
      with alphabet size at most k, it is NP-hard to distinguish between the case that val(Ψ) = 1 and
      the case that val(Ψ) ⩽ ε. This confirms the 4-to-1 Games Conjecture from [Khot, CCC 2002 ].
      Previously, the best known result, due to [Dinur, Khot, Kindler, Minzer, Safra], established the
      almost-perfect completeness version (but applied to the stricter problem of 2-to-1 games). Using
      results from the literature, we get the following implications:
       (1) For all k ∈ N, given a 3-colorable graph G, it is NP-hard to find a proper k-coloring.
       (2) For all δ > 0, given a 2-colorable 3-uniform hypergraph G, it is NP-hard to find in it an
           independent set containing at least δ fraction of the vertices.
         Our proof is a three-step construction that builds on the two-step framework of [Dinur,
      Khot, Kindler, Minzer, Safra]. In the outer-PCP step, we use quadratic equations to gain
      perfect completeness. We then construct a new middle PCP that performs low-rank tests while
      preserving a key covering property. Finally, we construct a new inner PCP based on a tensor of
      the standard Grassmann encoding with its low-rank variant due to [Golowich, FOCS 2023].

                                                   3
1        Introduction
The PCP Theorem [FGL+ 96, AS98, ALM+ 98] is a fundamental result in theoretical computer
science with many consequences. Most relevant to the current paper are the applications in the
area of hardness of approximation, in which the PCP theorem often serves as the starting point
for reductions. The combination of Fourier analysis with the long-code (or, in some cases, other
error correcting codes) has proven itself to be a very successful recipe, leading to many strong
hardness of approximation results [Fei98, Hås01, DS05, Zuc07]. However, this paradigm appears to
be incapable of establishing optimal results for many other problems, and below are a few notable
examples:
 (1) The Max-Cut problem: given a graph G = (V, E), the goal is to partition the vertex set
     into two sides A, B so as to maximize the number of edges crossing between A and B. A
     polynomial time algorithm due to [GW95] achieves the approximation ratio αGW ≈ 0.878,
                                                               16
     and the best known NP-hardness result [TSSW00] has ratio 17  + ε, for any ε > 0.
 (2) The Vertex-Cover problem: given a graph G = (V, E), the goal is to find the smallest set
     of vertices A ⊆ V that touches all edges of E. Known polynomial time algorithms achieve
     approximation ratio                                                      +         +
                       √ 2, and the best known NP-hardness result [KMS25, DKK 25, DKK 21,
     KMS23] has ratio 2 − ε, for any ε > 0.
 (3) The 3-coloring problem: given a graph G = (V, E) promised to be 3-colorable, the goal is to
     find a proper k-coloring of the vertices of G with k as small as possible. Polynomial time
     algorithms for this problem have been studied extensively over the years [Wig83, KMS98,
     BK97, ACC06, Chl07, KT17, KTY24, BHL26], and the state-of-the-art algorithm achieves
     k = |V |0.2−ε for a small explicit ε > 0. The NP-hardness of this problem has also been
     studied [KLS00, GK04, BBKO21], and the state-of-the-art NP-hardness result is for k = 5.
To make progress on these problems, Khot [Kho02b] introduced the Unique-Games Conjecture and
the d-to-1 Games conjectures, which we present next.

1.1        Label-Cover, Unique Games and d-to-1 Games
It is convenient to discuss PCPs in the language of the label-cover problem, defined as follows.
Definition 1.1. An instance Ψ of the label-cover problem consists of a bipartite graph G =
(L ⊔ R, E),1 alphabets ΣL , ΣR , and a collection of constraints {Φe ⊆ ΣL × ΣR }e∈E . For any given
positive integers d and d′ , a constraint Φe ⊆ ΣL × ΣR is called a d-to-d′ constraint if there is a
partition ΣL = ΣL,1 ⊔ · · · ⊔ ΣL,t into sets of size d and a partition ΣR = ΣR,1 ⊔ · · · ⊔ ΣR,t into sets
of size d′ such that
                                                [ t
                                          Φe =      ΣL,i × ΣR,i .
                                                         i=1
        Given a label-cover instance Ψ and assignments AL : L → ΣL , AR : R → ΣR , we define
                                                |{e = (u, v) ∈ E | (AL (u), AR (v)) ∈ Φe }|
                            valΨ (AL , AR ) =                                               ,
                                                                    |E|
that is, the fraction of constraints that are satisfied by AL , AR . We define the value of Ψ as
                                             val(Ψ) = max valΨ (AL , AR ).
                                                        AL ,AR
    1
        We always allow parallel edges, so E is a multi-set.


                                                               4
    For parameters 0 < s < c ⩽ 1, an input to the promise problem GapLC(c, s) is a label cover
instance Ψ that either has val(Ψ) ⩾ c or val(Ψ) ⩽ s, and the goal is to distinguish between the two
cases. The basic PCP theorem [FGL+ 96, AS98, ALM+ 98] combined with the parallel repetition
theorem [Raz98] gives:
Theorem 1.2. For all ε > 0, there exists a positive integer r such that GapLCr (1, ε) is NP-hard.
Here GapLCr is the GapLC problem in which both alphabets have size at most r.
    Khot conjectured that versions of Theorem 1.2 continue to hold even when the constraints are
d-to-1 for a small d. Towards presenting these conjectures, we make the following definition.
Definition 1.3. Let d and d′ be positive integers. An instance Ψ of label-cover is called a d-to-d′
game if all of its constraints are d-to-d′ . In the case that d = d′ = 1, Ψ is called a unique-
game. We write GapUGr (c, s) for the restriction of GapLCr (c, s) to unique-game instances, and
Gap-d-to-d′r (c, s) for its restriction to d-to-d′ game instances.
   It is easy to see that one cannot hope for Theorem 1.2 to hold for unique-games, and Khot
[Kho02b] conjectured that allowing for imperfect completeness remedies that:
Conjecture 1.4. For any ε > 0, there exists r ∈ N+ , such that GapUGr (1 − ε, ε) is NP-hard.
   For d ⩾ 2 and d′ = 1, he conjectured that the assertion of Theorem 1.2 should hold as is. More
precisely:
Conjecture 1.5. For any ε > 0 and any integer d ⩾ 2, there exists a positive integer r such that
Gap-d-to-1r (1, ε) is NP-hard.
    These conjectures, and especially Conjecture 1.4, have been shown to have remarkable impli-
cations, see for example [KR08, KKMO07, Rag08, KV15] and [Kho10b, Kho10a, Tre12, Kho14]
for surveys, and significant effort has gone into studying them. On the algorithmic front, connec-
tions with semi-definite programming, sum-of-squares algorithms and graph expansion have led to
the development of very powerful techniques and a number of algorithms for unique-games with
non-trivial guarantees [CMM06, AKK+ 08, ABS15]. On the hardness front, a line of work [KMS25,
DKK+ 25, DKK+ 21, KMS23] (with contributions from [BKS19, KMMS25]) established the imper-
fect completeness version of Conjecture 1.5, showing that for all ε > 0, there is r ∈ N+ such that
Gap-2-to-1r (1 − ε, ε) is NP-hard. This implies that for all ε > 0 there exists r ∈ N+ such that
GapUGr (1/2, ε) is NP-hard, which to date is the best evidence towards Conjecture 1.4.
    The difference between Conjecture 1.5 and its imperfect completeness version is significant in
a number of ways:
 (1) Implications: there are a number of hardness of approximation results that crucially rely
     on perfect completeness, most prominently graph coloring problems.
 (2) Techniques: the approach taken by [KMS25, DKK+ 25, DKK+ 21, KMS23] inherently in-
     curs imperfect completeness. Currently, constructions of low-soundness PCPs with per-
     fect completeness follow composition of PCPs, for example [MR10, DH13, DHK15], which
     at the very least require constructions that already have perfect completeness and small
     soundness (with perhaps a large alphabet which is then reduced), or from parallel repeti-
     tion [Raz98, Hol09, Rao11], which inherently increases the d-parameter rapidly.

1.2   Main Result
Our main result is that Conjecture 1.5 holds for d = 4 and d′ = 1.
Theorem 1.6. For all ε > 0, there exists r ∈ N+ , such that Gap-4-to-1r (1, ε) is NP-hard.

                                                5
1.2.1   Implications
We now discuss some implications of Theorem 1.6. Using the known connection between d-to-1
games and graph coloring/independent sets [DMR09, GS20], we get the following results.
Corollary 1.7. For any integer k ⩾ 3, given a graph G, it is NP-hard to distinguish between the
following two cases:
 (1) Yes case: G is 3-colorable.
 (2) No case: G is not k-colorable.
    Previously, Corollary 1.7 was known [BBKO21] for k = 5, or in the case the condition in the
yes-case only required a proper 3-coloring of 1 − ε fraction of the vertices [HMS23].
Remark 1.8. Under slightly super-polynomial time reductions, our techniques give versions of Corol-
lary 1.7 for k growing with the instance size, but at a very slow rate. Namely, assuming NP has no
algorithms running in time nt(n) , we get that there are no polynomial time algorithms that color a
given 3-colorable graph with O(log · · · log t(n)) colors, where the number of logs is finite (probably
at most 10, say).
   In the next corollary, the condition in the completeness case is weaker, but the condition in the
soundness case is stronger:
Corollary 1.9. For all δ > 0, given a graph G, it is NP-hard to distinguish between the following
two cases:
 (1) Yes case: G is 8-colorable.
 (2) No case: G has no independent set of relative size at least δ.
   Using [KS14], we get the following result for hypergraphs:
Corollary 1.10. For all δ > 0, given a 3-uniform hypergraph G, it is NP-hard to distinguish
between the following two cases:
 (1) Yes case: G is 2-colorable.
 (2) No case: G has no independent set of relative size at least δ.
   Using the results of [HMRW14], Corollaries 1.7 and 1.9 show hardness results for low-rank matrix
completion. An instance in the (k, r, ε, c)-completion problem is a matrix A ∈ (R ∪ {⊥})n×n , such
that there exists M ∈ Rn×n satisfying:
 (1) |M (i, j)| ⩽ c and M has rank at most r.
 (2) Letting Ω ⊆ [n] × [n] be the set of (i, j) such that A(i, j) ̸= ⊥, we have that |Ω| ⩾ 0.9n2 and
     for each (i, j) ∈ Ω we have M (i, j) = A(i, j).
The goal is to find a matrix B ∈ Rn×n of rank at most k, such that |B(i, j)| ⩽ c for all (i, j) and
                                  X
                                       |A(i, j) − B(i, j)|2 ⩽ εn.
                                   (i,j)∈Ω

Applying [HMRW14, Theorem 1.8] on Corollary 1.7 gives:
Corollary 1.11. For all k > 3 and c > 1, the (k, 3, 0, c)-completion problem is NP-hard.
   Applying [HMRW14, Theorem 1.9] on Corollary 1.9 gives:
Corollary 1.12. For all k > 8 and c > 1, the (k, 8, ε, c)-completion problem is NP-hard for all
           1
0 < ε < (2ck)2.



                                                  6
1.3   Our Techniques
In this section we give a brief account of the components that go into the proof of Theorem 1.6.
We refer the reader to Section 2 for a more thorough description.
    For 2-to-1 games with almost-perfect completeness, the proof of [KMS25, DKK+ 25, DKK+ 21,
KMS23] combines the following two components:

 (1) An outer PCP with completeness close to 1, vanishing soundness, and a feature called the
     “covering property” (originally appearing in [KS13]). This game is very far from being a
     2-to-1 game, and to turn it into a 2-to-1 game it is composed with an appropriate inner PCP.
     The goal of the covering property is to facilitate the composition step.

 (2) An inner PCP with perfect completeness and a non-standard notion of soundness. The
     inner PCP is based on the Grassmann graph, the Grassmann encoding and the Grassmann
     agreement test. The Grassmann agreement test can be viewed as a local tester for the
     Grassmann code, but it turns out not to be sound in the traditional error correcting codes
     sense. Instead, it satisfies a weaker notion of soundness, which we refer to as non-standard
     soundness.

The outer PCP and inner PCP are then composed, and the covering property of the outer PCP is
used in the soundness analysis of the composed PCP (even though the inner PCP only satisfies a
non-standard notion of soundness).
    The outer PCP construction of [KMS25, DKK+ 25, DKK+ 21, KMS23] is based on the hardness
of approximation result of [Hås01] for solving linear equations, which inherently has imperfect
completeness, and this propagates throughout their construction. To remedy that, a key difficulty
is to come up with an outer PCP that has perfect completeness and simultaneously admits (some
version of) the covering property.
    We start our outer PCP construction based on hardness of solving a system of quadratic equa-
tions (which has perfect completeness). However, the Grassmann agreement test (which is still
basically the only tool we have to achieve d-to-1-ness in the inner PCP) is inherently a “linear” test
and cannot incorporate quadratic equations. In order for quadratic equations to be accommodated
in a d-to-1 way, we add a layer of construction called the “middle PCP” between the outer and inner
PCPs. In the middle PCP, we carry out a low-rank test that allows us to convert the quadratic
equations into linear ones.
    However, this linearization of quadratic equations destroys the covering property that is essential
for composing with a Grassmann-based inner PCP. To remedy that, we have to come up with
another two key ideas:

 (1) We do a “lazy parallel repetition” in the middle PCP. This creates room for covering properties
     while preserving the soundness.

 (2) The covering property we get from lazy parallel repetition is still not sufficient for com-
     posing with the Grassmann agreement test of [KMS25, DKK+ 25, DKK+ 21, KMS23]. As a
     workaround, we base our inner PCP on a combination of standard Grassmann graphs over
     matrix spaces Fn×n
                     2    with a sparser version of them, known as the low-rank Grassmann graph
     over Fn×n
           2   , recently defined by [Gol23].

Statement of AI use: the mathematical ideas and the overall approach presented in this paper
are due to the authors. ChatGPT 5.6, 6 and 6.1 were used for polishing and proofreading.



                                                  7
2     Proof Overview
In this section we give an overview of the proof of Theorem 1.6. It will be useful to first discuss
the construction of [KMS25, DKK+ 25, DKK+ 21, KMS23] which achieves imperfect completeness.
We then discuss why it fails to have perfect completeness, and then in Section 2.2 we explain
preliminary attempts at fixing that, building towards our construction.

2.1     The Imperfect Completeness Construction
In this section we explain the components that go into the construction of [KMS25, DKK+ 25,
DKK+ 21, KMS23].

2.1.1    The Outer PCP
It will often be easier for us to describe label-cover instances in the equivalent, active language of
2-prover-1-round games, which we introduce next. In a 2-prover-1-round game, a verifier samples
a challenge e = (u, v), sends u to a prover named Alice, sends v to a prover named Bob. Upon
receiving their challenges, they respond with labels (they are not allowed to communicate once they
get their challenges), and based on their responses the verifier decides to accept or reject. The value
of the game is the maximum, over all strategies of Alice and Bob, of the probability that the verifier
accepts. Identifying the individual challenges the players receive as vertices of a bipartite graph,
pairs of challenges the verifier may sample with its edges, and the verifier’s acceptance criteria with
the constraints, one gets the equivalence. It is easy to see that the value of a game is exactly the
value of the resulting label cover instance.
    With this in mind, the reduction of [KMS25, DKK+ 25, DKK+ 21, KMS23] starts with the result
of [Hås01] regarding the approximability of 3LinF2 .
Definition 2.1. An instance of the 3LinF2 problem consists of a set of variables V and a set of
equations E , where each e ∈ E is a linear equation over F2 containing 3 distinct variables.
    Håstad proved that for all ε > 0, Gap3LinF2 (1 − ε, 1/2 + ε) is NP-hard, and the reduction
of [KMS25, DKK+ 25, DKK+ 21, KMS23] starts off with (some regularized version of) this problem.
More specifically, they use the parallel repetition theorem to prove NP-hardness for a certain type
of label-cover instance Ψq,β,k = Ψq,β,k (V , E ), which we present below in the active 2-prover-1-round
game view, and refer to as the β-smooth k-fold repeated game. For technical reasons, this game
contains an additional feature called “advice”, which we encourage the reader to ignore in a first
reading.
    1. Alice’s challenge: the verifier samples equations e1 , . . . , ek from E uniformly and indepen-
       dently, and sets E to be the set of all variables that appear in them.
    2. Bob’s challenge: for each i ∈ {1, . . . , k} independently, with probability 1 − β, the verifier
       takes Ei′ to be the set of variables in ei , and with probability β the verifier chooses Ei′ to
       consist of a single uniformly chosen variable from ei . The verifier sets E ′ = E1′ ∪ . . . ∪ Ek′ .
                                                           ′
    3. Advice: the verifier chooses b(1) , . . . , b(q) ∈ FE                              (1)
                                                             2 uniformly, and then takes a , . . . , a
                                                                                                       (q) ∈ FE
                                                                                                              2
                    (i)                    (i)
       where a is obtained from b by filling             the missing coordinates with 0’s.  We   write    B  =
        b(1) , . . . , b(q) and A = a(1) , . . . , a(q) .

    4. Acceptance criteria: the verifier sends (E, A) to Alice, and (E ′ , B) to Bob. They respond
       with vectors x ∈ FE                  E′
                          2 and y ∈ F2 respectively, and the verifier accepts if and only if x satisfies
       all of the equations e1 , . . . , ek and x|E ′ = y.

                                                       8
The works [KMS25, DKK+ 25, DKK+ 21, KMS23] prove that for all ε > 0, it is NP-hard to
distinguish between the case that val(Ψq,β,k ) ⩾ 1 − ε, and the case that val(Ψq,β,k ) ⩽ 2−Ωq (βk) .
Here and throughout, one should think of q as an absolute constant, and of β as slightly larger
than 1/k, say β = k −3/4 . The game Ψq,β,k has several useful features in the context of 2-to-1
Games:

 (1) Simplicity of the constraints: the constraints of Ψq,β,k can all be seen to be linear equa-
     tions in the responses of the provers. This is because the starting point, the 3LinF2 , consisted
     of linear constraints, and on top of that we have the consistency constraints between the
     responses of Alice and Bob, which are also linear.
                                                                     ′
 (2) The covering property: for a fixed E, the spaces FE       2 living on Bob’s side cover Alice’s
     space F2 almost perfectly. For example, fixing E, taking E ′ randomly as described above
             E
                                    ′
     conditioned on E, then y ∈ FE                           E
                                   2 and lifting it to x ∈ F2 by filling in the missing coordinates
     with 0’s, we have that the distribution of x is statistically close to the uniform distribution
     over FE
           2 (this property first appeared in [KS13] in a somewhat different context).

The relevance of these properties will become clear in the next part, the inner PCP.

2.1.2   The Inner PCP
The 2-prover 1-round game Ψq,β,k constructed in Section 2.1.1 is far from 2-to-1, because given a
                     ′
Bob-answer y ∈ FE                                                       E
                    2 , there are many possible Alice-answers x ∈ F2 consistent with it. In order
to make it a 2-to-1 game, in a single query we can ask the provers only for a smaller piece of
information.
    For each possible question (E, A) to Alice, we ask her to prepare her answer in “Grassmann
encoding,” and instead of requesting a full answer x ∈ FE
                                                        2 , we ask her to return only the information
in one location of the Grassmann encoding of x.
    The Grassmann encoding scheme is based on the Grassmann graph Gr(FE              2 , ℓ), whose vertices
                                              E                            ′
are the ℓ-dimensional linear subspaces of F2 . Two vertices L and L are adjacent if and only if
dim(L ∩ L′ ) = ℓ − 1. By a slight abuse of notation, we also use Gr(FE    2 , ℓ) to denote the vertex set
of this graph. The Grassmann encoding of a vector x ∈ F2 stores, at each location L ∈ Gr(FE
                                                            E
                                                                                                     2 , ℓ),
the local linear function
                                F [L] : L → F2 defined by z 7→ ⟨z, x⟩ .
                                                           ′
   Similarly, we ask Bob to prepare his answer y ∈ FE
                                                    2 in Grassmann encoding, where each location
 ′       E ′
L ∈ Gr(F2 , ℓ − 1) stores the local function

                                 G[L′ ] : L′ → F2 defined by z 7→ ⟨z, y⟩ .

    The final 2-to-1 game is the composition of the outer PCP with the Grassmann graph gadget.
In the composed PCP, upon choosing (E, A) and (E ′ , B) as in Ψq,β,k , the verifier chooses a space
                   ′                               E′                                              E′
L′ from Gr(FE                                                          ′
                 2 , ℓ − 1) and a space L ∈ Gr(F2 , ℓ) containing L . The subspace L ⊆ F2 will be
regarded as a subspace of Alice’s space FE      2 by appending 0’s in the missing coordinates. The
verifier then sends (E, A, L) to Alice and (E ′ , B, L′ ) to Bob, expecting to get linear functions
F [E, A, L] : L → F2 and G[E ′ , B, L′ ] : L′ → F2 . The verifier accepts only if G[E ′ , B, L′ ] agrees with
the restriction F [E, A, L]|L′ . This is essentially a 2-to-1 game because for any possible choice of
G[E ′ , B, L′ ], there are at most two choices of F [E, A, L] consistent with it.




                                                     9
Covering property and consistency: consider a question (E, A) for Alice in the outer PCP
(think of ℓ as much smaller than k), and the Grassmann graph Gr(FE        2 , ℓ). The covering property
                                                                                                    ′
above asserts that if we sample E ′ as in the outer PCP and a vertex in the graph Gr(FE            2 , ℓ),
lifting it to Gr(FE
                  2 , ℓ) yields a nearly uniform vertex in the latter graph. This implies that if Alice’s
assignment F [E, A, ·] has agreement ε′ with an actual Grassmann codeword F , then with decent
                                                                                             ′
probability over E ′ , Bob’s assignment G[E ′ , B, ·] agrees with the projection of F to FE2 . In short,
the covering property ensures the consistency between decodings of assignments of the provers
holds.

The linear equations side conditions: recall that in the outer PCP we want to ensure that
Alice’s response x ∈ FE   2 satisfies a collection of k linear equations. Note that these equations
correspond to requiring that the function z 7→ ⟨z, x⟩ attains specific values on k inputs. More
precisely, letting the equations be e1 , . . . , ek , letting s(i) ∈ FE
                                                                      2 be the indicator vector of the variables
appearing in ei , and letting ti ∈ F2 be the constant term of the equation ei , we want to ensure
that s(i) , x = ti . To do so, we define the space NE = span(s(1) , . . . , s(k) ), and request Alice
to give assignment F [E, A, L + NE ] : (L + NE ) → F2 (instead of just L → F2 ). The verifier then
checks consistency between Alice and Bob similarly to the above, and additionally that F [E, A, L +
NE ]|NE = gE , where the linear function gE : NE → F2 is defined by gE (s(i) ) = ti .

2.1.3    On the Non-Standard Notion of Soundness
One would wish that the Grassmann gadget in the inner PCP has the following type of soundness:
suppose F [L] is a linear map L → F2 for each L ∈ Gr(FE      2 , ℓ), such that
                            h                                                      i
                      P       F [L1 ]|L1 ∩L2 = F [L2 ]|L1 ∩L2 dim(L1 ∩ L2 ) = ℓ − 1 ⩾ δ  (2.1)
                L1 ,L2 ∈Gr(FE
                            2 ,ℓ)


for some constant δ ∈ (0, 1), then there exists a linear function f : FE
                                                                       2 → F2 such that
                                              h             i
                                        P       F [L] = f |L ⩾ δ ′
                                            L∈Gr(FE
                                                  2 ,ℓ)


for some δ ′ ∈ (0, 1). However, this conclusion cannot be made, as we next explain. Take ε > 0 to
be a small constant, and take a random set X ⊆ FE                    −ℓ    |E|
                                                    2 \ {0} of size 2 ε · 2 . For each x ∈ X choose
a random linear function fx : FE2 → F2 , and set a table F on ℓ-dimensional subspaces L as follows:

   • If L contains a unique point x ∈ X, define F [L] = fx |L .
   • Otherwise, choose F [L] as a random linear function on L.
Using the inclusion-exclusion formula, one can prove that for a random edge {L1 , L2 } in the Grass-
mann graph, with probability at least Ω(ε) there is x ∈ X such that L1 ∩ X = L2 ∩ X = {x}, and
F satisfies such constraints. Thus, F passes the Grassmann test Equation (2.1) with δ = Ω(ε).
However, it only agrees on o(1) locations with any given codeword from the Grassmann code.
    This example tells us that while there might be no linear function that agrees with F globally,
it may be possible to extract a linear function by focusing on certain substructures inside the
Grassmann graph. Indeed, it turns out that what can be concluded from Equation (2.1) is something
of this form: for at least exp(−O(ℓ2 )) fraction of Q ∈ Gr(FE
                                                            2 , q), where q depends only on δ, there
                 E
exists W ∈ Gr(F2 , |E| − q) and a linear function f : W → F2 such that
                                       h                          i
                                 P       F [L] ≡ f |L Q ⊆ L ⊆ W ⩾ δ ′ .                        (2.2)
                                    L∈Gr(FE
                                          2 ,ℓ)



                                                          10
We refer to the spaces Q and W in Equation (2.2) as zoom-ins and zoom-outs, respectively, and
encourage the reader to ignore W (it will be irrelevant for the majority of the discussion). In
words, Equation (2.2) asserts that if F passes the Grassmann test with noticeable probability, then
inside non-trivial fraction of zoom-ins of constant dimension q, it does have a global linear structure.
    The proof of [KMS25, DKK+ 25, DKK+ 21, KMS23] has to deal with this non-standard notion
of soundness, and this is the purpose of the advice feature in the game Ψq,β,k . More precisely,
given Alice and Bob’s tables of assignments F and G for the composed PCP, one needs to be able
to derive a strategy for the original game Ψq,β,k . However, the strategy that one can derive using
Equation (2.2) heavily depends on the choice of what zoom-in Q is used. The advice feature allows
the two provers in Ψq,β,k to use (essentially) the same Q for decoding — namely the span of their
advice vectors — which guarantees consistency between their answers.

2.1.4    Difficulties in Achieving Perfect Completeness
The construction for 2-to-1 games with imperfect completeness can be illustrated using Figure 1.

                  Outer PCP
         1. equation vs. variable game
                                                                         Inner PCP
         2. smooth parallel repetition
                                                                   Grassmann agreement test
               (for low soundness
             and covering property)

  Figure 1: The imperfect-completeness construction of [KMS25, DKK+ 25, DKK+ 21, KMS23]

    The above construction loses perfect completeness right from the start: it uses the 3LinF2 prob-
lem, which inherently has imperfect completeness. Moreover, in some sense this seems necessary:
the Grassmann encoding presented above only allows for linear checks in the encoded string x ∈ Fn2 .
    Thus, to achieve perfect completeness it is necessary to come up with an encoding scheme which
is more powerful than the Grassmann encoding, in the sense that it allows for non-linear checks
on the encoded string x ∈ FE  2 . By itself this is not a hard task; for example, the long-code would
work. However, the key difficulty is that one additionally needs the encoding scheme to satisfy a
covering property, and then build an outer PCP with a suitable advice feature.

2.2     Our Techniques
In this section we discuss the new ideas that go into our construction.

2.2.1    A First Attempt: the Quadratic Grassmann Encoding
We begin by outlining a natural first attempt at a reduction achieving perfect completeness. It
starts with the problem of quadratic equations, 3QSF2 , defined as follows.
Definition 2.2. An instance of the 3QSF2 problem consists of a set of variables V and a set of
equations E , where each e ∈ E is a quadratic equation over F2 containing 3 distinct variables.
    Using standard results, it can be proved that there is s < 1 such that Gap3QSF2 [1, s] is NP-
hard. Starting with an instance (V , E ) of 3QSF2 , one can construct the β-smooth k-fold repeated
game corresponding to it, Ψβ,k = Ψβ,k (V , E ) in the same way as in Section 2.1.1 (we ignore the
advice for now). The main question is how to compose it with a Grassmann-style encoding so as
to get to a d-to-1 Game.

                                                  11
   In the game Ψβ,k , the verifier performs two types of checks: consistency between the responses
of Alice and Bob, and quadratic equations in the response of Alice. The Grassmann encoding is
compatible with the former type of checks, but not with the latter type of checks, in the sense that
we do not know how to incorporate checking quadratic equations on the encoded message into the
Grassmann encoding itself. Instead, it is natural to consider the quadratic Grassmann encoding.
   Let x ∈ FE2 represent Alice’s response in the game Ψβ,k . In the quadratic Grassmann encoding,
one considers the functions over F2E×E defined by z 7→ ⟨z, x ⊗ x⟩, and takes its Grassmann encoding
QF of x, which assigns to each ℓ-dimensional subspace L ⊆ FE×E  2    the linear function
                                QF [L] : L → F2 defined by z 7→ ⟨z, x ⊗ x⟩ .
The main benefit of the quadratic Grassmann encoding is that it indeed allows us to test quadratic
equations as side conditions, as we explain next.

The quadratic equations side conditions: Given legitimate quadratic Grassmann codeword
QF , it is possible to incorporate with it checks of quadratic equations on the encoded message.
Indeed, suppose that e1 , . . . , ek are quadratic equations over E, let s(1) , . . . , s(k) ∈ FE×E     2    be the
indicator vectors of the pairs that appear in e1 , . . . , ek respectively, and let t1 , . . . , tk be the constant
terms. Then the equation ei can be written as si , x ⊗ x = ti for each i ∈ {1, . . . , k}. As before,
this can be folded into the encoding by defining NE = span(s(1) , . . . , s(k) ), asking for the table to
assign values to the sum L + NE (instead of to just L), and require that their projection onto
NE completely agrees with the linear function g : NE → F2 defined by g(s(i) ) = ti . This way of
incorporating quadratic side conditions will work for all of the constructions we present below, so
for simplicity of presentation they will be omitted henceforth.
    On the other hand, the quadratic Grassmann encoding has 2 very significant drawbacks:
 (1) Codeword testing: Even ignoring the issue of non-standard-type soundness, the Grassmann
     agreement test from Section 2.1.2 is not actually a tester for the quadratic Grassmann code.
     Namely, if a table G that assigns each ℓ-dimensional subspace L ⊆ FE×E  2    a linear function
     G[L] : L → F2 passes it with non-negligible probability, then at best we will be able to argue
     that it has significant agreement with the Grassmann encoding of an arbitrary x′ ∈ FE×E  2    ,
     i.e., not necessarily of rank 1. We need an additional feature that will guarantee that x′ has
     rank 1 (or at the very least small rank) and is symmetric.
 (2) The covering property: The covering property does not hold for the Grassmann encoding.
     Namely, fixing a question for Alice E and looking at typical possible questions E ′ for Bob
                                                        ′ ×E ′
     (conditioned on E), the (lifting of the) spaces FE                 E×E
                                                      2  do not cover F2   . Indeed, the number
                                                       3k
     of such typical questions for Bob is roughly 2βk , and the size of Bob’s space is roughly
              2 2
      2(3−2β) k , so in total they can cover at most
                                 
                               3k         2 2                     2    2       2
                                   2(3−2β) k ⩽ 2O(βk log(1/β)) 29k −8βk = o(29k )
                              2βk
      points in FE×E
                 2   , which is a tiny fraction.
The first issue, namely ensuring that the encoded message has a low rank, is significantly easier to
handle, and to do so we incorporate tensor tests. More precisely, instead of asking for the value of
the Grassmann encoding at a subspace L ⊆ FE×E  2    , we will also take a random subspace U ⊆ FE2 of
dimension ℓ, and then ask for the value at the subspace L + U ⊗ U , requiring that the projection
onto U ⊗ U is of rank 1. This yields the following 4-to-4 Grassmann agreement + tensor test. Upon
receiving a table F assigning linear functions to spaces L + U ⊗ U as above, do the following:

                                                        12
   1. Sample L1 , L2 ⊆ FE×E
                         2    of dimension ℓ intersecting at dimension ℓ − 1, and U1 , U2 ⊆ FE
                                                                                             2 of
      dimension ℓ intersecting at dimension ℓ − 1.

   2. Query F [L1 + U1 ⊗ U1 ] and F [L2 + U2 ⊗ U2 ]. Accept if they agree on

                                       L1 ∩ L2 + (U1 ∩ U2 ) ⊗ (U1 ∩ U2 ),

        and their respective projections onto U1 ⊗ U1 and U2 ⊗ U2 are symmetric and of rank at most
        1.
Given the (non-standard) soundness of the basic Grassmann agreement test, one can analyze the
Grassmann agreement + tensor test and show that it satisfies a similar (non-standard) notion of
soundness. Also, this test is 4-to-4: given a linear function on L1 , there are at most 2 linear
functions on L2 that agree with it, and given a symmetric rank-1 functional on U1 ⊗ U1 , there are
at most 2 symmetric rank-1 linear functions on U2 ⊗ U2 agreeing with it.
    Ensuring symmetry is more straightforward. To do that, define for distinct points i, j ∈ E the
matrix Mi,j ∈ FE×E
                2     which is 1 only at locations (i, j) and (j, i), and set Nsym = span({Mi,j | i ̸= j}).
Then, x′ ∈ FE×E
             2    is symmetric   if and only if the function z 7→ ⟨z, x′ ⟩ vanishes on Nsym . Thus, instead
of requesting an assignment on L + U ⊗ U , the verifier requests an assignment on L + U ⊗ U + Nsym ,
and checks that its projection on Nsym vanishes.
    The second issue, namely the fact that the covering property fails, is substantially more difficult
to handle, and in the next section we begin the discussion about it.

2.2.2     Variations on the Quadratic Grassmann Encoding
                                                                                     ×E ′   ′
From now on, it will be convenient for us to think of points from FE×E  2    and FE2     as matrices.
The covering property fails for the quadratic Grassmann encoding because if, letting Bob’s question
be denoted by E ′ , we have i ∈ E \ E ′ , then i-th row and column will always be 0 in liftings from
    ′ ×E ′
FE2        to FE×E
               2   . On the other hand, typical matrices from FE×E
                                                                2   do not contain fully-0 rows. This
raises the question of whether we can replace the structure of E × E with a sparser structure that
circumvents this atypical behaviour of liftings. Moreover, requiring a response from the provers for
each pair of variables in their respective variable sets is not necessary, as the quadratic equations
we wish to check only require access to Θ(k) pairs (out of the k2 many ones that exist).
                                                                   

      This view motivates the following general recipe: can we find a graph H with vertex set E and
edge set E, such that we can restrict the quadratic Grassmann encoding to FE2 in a meaningful way?
The quadratic Grassmann encoding discussed above corresponds to choosing H to be a complete
graph, but there are many other options for H. Below are a few simple choices we tried:
 (1) Expander graphs: One can try to take H to be an arbitrary sparse expander graph. It can be
     shown that if the degree of H is small then now the covering property will hold. Unfortunately,
     an arbitrary expander graph does not support any form of tensor tests.

 (2) Cliques in a sparse high-dimensional expander: one can take a sparse high-dimensional ex-
     pander X = (X(0), . . . , X(k)), where X(0) is the vertex set, and for each i, X(i) ⊆ X(0)
                                                                                                  
                                                                                              i+1 ,
     and take H to be the graph underlying it. Because the degree of X is constant, the covering
     property holds. Also, we can perform local type of tensor tests: if K ∈ X(k) is a (k + 1)-face,
                                                                   X(0)
     then K is a clique in X, so we can choose a subspace U ⊆ F2         fully supported in K, and
     have access to all of the entries in U ⊗ U .
        This observation initially seems very promising, but it has one serious issue. We can only
        afford to perform such a local tensor test for a single clique (or perhaps O(1)-many cliques if we

                                                    13
        are fine with increasing the value of d for which we are proving Conjecture 1.5). Unfortunately,
        these localized tensor tests do not actually guarantee that the encoded function has a small
        rank, and Alice and Bob can use a cheating strategy in the following way. When Alice receives
        a space of the type L + U ⊗ U , she extracts from it a rank-1 vector, and takes any element v in
        its support. Then it is highly likely that v belongs to K, and because X has constant degree,
        the neighbourhood N (v) of v has constant size. Thus, Alice can choose a linear function on
          X(1)                       X(1)∩(N (v)×N (v))
        F2     which is rank-1 on F2                    but otherwise arbitrary, and responds according
        to it. Bob proceeds in the same way, where they agree beforehand on what is the rank-1
        function they will use, and what the arbitrary function they will use on the rest of the space.
        We remark that another attempt at implementing tensor tests via high-dimensional expanders
        is based on coboundary-type expansion, and we have not explored this thoroughly.

In summary, the attempts above suggest that there are meaningful variants of the quadratic Grass-
mann encoding that enjoy the covering property, but they come at the cost of, at best, being able
to ensure small rank locally. This type of guarantee seems insufficient, at least with an outer PCP
along the lines of Section 2.1.1.

                  Outer PCP                    not enough                  Inner PCP
         1. equation vs. variable game      covering property     1. Grassmann agreement test
         2. smooth parallel repetition                                    2. tensor test
               (for low soundness                                    3. expander variations?
             and covering property)                                  (for covering property)

                         Figure 2: Our failed attempt for perfect completeness

2.2.3     A Second Attempt: Three-Step Construction
So far, the described attempts to create covering property for a quadratic-Grassmann-based in-
ner PCP are not successful. In particular, it seems that the idea of “sparsifying” the quadratic-
Grassmann encoding conflicts with tensor test. To address this conflict, we separate the tensor test
from the inner PCP and name it the “middle PCP” (see Figure 3). Notice that the tensor test
                                                                                   E×E
(proposed in Section 2.2.1) is a Grassmann structure over FE  2 , instead of over F2   . Therefore,
the tensor test by itself is compatible with the covering property provided by the smooth parallel
repetition in the outer PCP. We only have to create more covering property for the inner PCP
(which now consists only of a Grassmann agreement test) after incorporating tensor tests into the
middle PCP. This will be provided by a “lazy parallel repetition” on the level of the middle PCP
(see Figure 3).

            Outer PCP
                                               Middle PCP
   1. equation vs. variable game                                              Inner PCP
                                               1. tensor test
   2. smooth parallel repetition                                        Grassmann agreement test
                                         2. lazy parallel repetition
         (for low soundness                                              with low-rank variation
                                          (for covering property)
       and covering property)

                          Figure 3: Our construction for perfect completeness




                                                   14
2.2.4       Our Outer PCP
Besides using quadratic equations to replace linear ones, the main difference between our outer
PCP and the one in Section 2.1.1 is that we need to establish a stronger soundness condition. This
is because the tensor tests we ultimately perform will only guarantee that the decoded assignment
has low rank (as opposed to rank-1).
    Given a collection of quadratic equations (V , E ) over F2 and a solution x ∈ FV2 , one can consider
the rank-1 (or 0) matrix x ⊗ x ∈ FV2 ×V and view it as an assignment to (V , E ). This proposes a
broader set of solutions given by low-rank matrices, motivating the following definition:

Definition 2.3. Let e be a quadratic equation over V described by ⟨x, Ax⟩ = b for a matrix
A ∈ FV2 ×V , and let x(1) , . . . , x(r) ∈ FV2 be assignments. We say that x(1) , . . . , x(r) jointly satisfy e if
                                             r
                                             X X
                                                         Auv x(i) (i)
                                                              u xv = b.
                                             i=1 u,v∈V

   We remark that [KS17] use the same notion, which they refer to as “superposition complexity”.
With this definition in mind, the starting point of our reduction is the following result.2

Theorem 2.4. For all r ∈ N+ , there exists ε > 0 such that the following holds. Given an instance
Ψ = Ψ(V , E ) of 3QSF2 , it is NP-hard to distinguish between the following two cases:

 (1) Yes case: val(Ψ) = 1.

 (2) No case: for all x(1) , . . . , x(r) ∈ FV2 , we have that x(1) , . . . , x(r) jointly satisfy at most 1 − ε
     fraction of the equations in E .

    As in Section 2.1.1, we can view the instances in Theorem 2.4 as an equation vs. variable game,
and then apply a β-smooth k-fold parallel repetition to get a game Ψq,β,k .
    The soundness condition in Theorem 2.4 translates to a variant of Ψq,β,k where we ask the
provers to return r vectors. In particular, upon receiving a question (E, A), Alice needs to return
x(1) , . . . , x(r) ∈ FE
                       2 that jointly satisfy all the equations in E. It turns out that one can modify
this game into a different game where we ask the provers return low-rank symmetric matrices. In
particular, Alice now needs to return a symmetric matrix X ∈ FE×E     2    that satisfies the equations in
E, in the sense that for each e in E described by ⟨x, Ax⟩ = b, we have
                                                X
                                                     Auv Xuv = b.
                                                u,v∈E

In Section 4.4, we will show that this conversion from joint satisfaction to low-rank-matrix answers
does not affect the soundness by too much.
    For the purpose of composing with the middle and inner PCPs, we view an Alice-answer X ∈
FE×E
 2    as a symmetric bilinear map fX : FE        E
                                           2 ⊗ F2 → F2 defined by
                                        X

                                                          15
2.2.5     Our Middle PCP
In the outer PCP described in Section 2.2.4, the verifier (roughly speaking) carries out the following
(cf. Section 2.1.1):
  1. Sample (E, A) and send it to Alice. Sample (E ′ , B) and send it to Bob.

  2. Receive from Alice a symmetric bilinear map f : FE    E
                                                      2 ⊗ F2 → F2 of rank at most r, and from
                                        E ′   E ′
     Bob a symmetric bilinear map g : F2 ⊗ F2 → F2 .
                                           ′       ′
  3. Accept if the restriction of f to FE    E
                                        2 ⊗ F2 is identical to g.


Local tensor check: the first goal of the middle PCP is to replace the global rank check
“rank(f ) ⩽ r” with a local tensor check. We move to the game Ψmid where the verifier carries
out the following:
  1. Sample (E, A) and (E ′ , B) as in the outer PCP game Ψq,β,k .
                                      ′                   ′                  ′
  2. Sample a subspace U ∈ Gr(FE                                   E
                                 2 , ℓ2 ) and a subspace U ∈ Gr(F2 , ℓ2 + 1) containing U . The
                     ′
     subspace U ⊆ FE                                                   E
                    2 will be regarded as a subspace of Alice’s space F2 by appending 0’s in the
     missing coordinates.

  3. Send (E, A, U ) to Alice and (E ′ , B, U ′ ) to Bob.

  4. Receive from Alice a symmetric bilinear map f : FE
                                                      2 ⊗  FE
                                                            2 → F2 such that rank(f |U ⊗U ) ⩽ 1,
                                                E ′    E ′
     and from Bob a symmetric bilinear map g : F2 ⊗ F2 → F2 .
                                           ′       ′
  5. Accept if the restriction of f to FE    E
                                        2 ⊗ F2 is identical to g.

     In words, we have replaced the global rank check on Alice’s response with a local rank check,
that verifies that the rank of f on U ⊗ U is at most 1, where U is a (nearly uniformly chosen)
ℓ2 -dimensional subspace given to Alice.
     The completeness of this transformation is clear, but the soundness is much less so. Indeed,
because we provide U to Alice, she knows where the rank test will be performed, and therefore she
can potentially use that information to cheat. Nevertheless, we show that for all δ > 0, if we fix
r, q, ℓ2 appropriately and let k be sufficiently large, then in the “no case” of Theorem 2.4 we have
val(Ψmid ) ⩽ δ. The intuition here is as follows:
 (1) If Alice did not use any cheating strategy, but her f : FE    E
                                                              2 ⊗ F2 → F2 was of rank more than
     r, then the probability that f |U ×U has rank at most 1 approaches 0 as ℓ2 goes to infinity.

 (2) The type of cheating strategies Alice may use is limited. To see that, fix strategies F, G for
     Alice and Bob that achieve value at least s, fix the question of Bob, and sample two questions
     for Alice that only differ on the tensor test, say (E, A, U1 ) and (E, A, U2 ). Using the Cauchy-
     Schwarz inequality, we get that with probability at least s2 , Alice and Bob win both of these
     challenges. In that case, Alice’s functions F [E, A, U1 ], F [E, A, U2 ] have to agree with Bob’s
     function, which is the same in both scenarios (his challenge has not changed). In particular,
     in that scenario we must have that

                                F [E, A, U1 ]|FE′ ⊗FE′ = F [E, A, U2 ]|FE′ ⊗FE′ .
                                               2       2                2        2


        In words, fixing (E, A), we get that Alice’s responses (as a function of the tensor test U
        she receives) must pass a sort of Grassmann agreement test. If that test had the standard

                                                       16
      notion of soundness, it would mean that Alice’s responses have to basically follow an honest
      strategy, and a version of the argument from the first item will further guarantee that this
      honest strategy uses a low-rank function.

 (3) Using the non-standard notion of soundness (which we know to hold), the discussion in Sec-
     tion 2.1.3 implies that after an appropriate zoom-in (and zoom-out, which we ignore for now),
     Alice’s responses do behave like an honest strategy, and one can argue as in the first item.
     The implementation of the zoom-ins in the soundness analysis of Ψmid uses the advice A.

Lazy parallel repetition: next, we wish to turn the check that f |FE′ ⊗FE′ = g from Ψmid into
                                                                             2    2
a 2-to-1 test, and towards this end we intend to use the Grassmann encoding. A naive approach
                                                                                                E×E
would have been to apply the Grassmann encoding directly on Alice’s space FE              E
                                                                                    2 ⊗ F2 = F2      and
                     ′      ′      ′
                                 E ×E ′
                   E      E
on Bob’s space F2 ⊗ F2 = F2             , but as discussed in Section 2.2.1 this fails due to the lack of
the covering property. Our solution for this issue is to first hide the actual challenge within a large
collection of trivial challenges, in an operation that we refer to as lazy repetition (we remark that
it has appeared in PCP literature previously, see [Kho02a]). In the terminology of Section 2.2.2,
this amounts to taking a graph H which is a union of a large number of cliques (with no edges
between them at all).
    To describe the operation, fix a parameter t thought of as much larger than any of the previously
mentioned parameters. Consider the game Ψlazy where the verifier carries out the following:

  1. Sample c ∈ [t], and sample challenges (Ec , Ac , Uc ), (Ec′ , Bc , Uc′ ) as in Ψmid . We refer to c as
     the critical index.

  2. For each j ∈ [t] \ {c}, sample an Alice-challenge (Ej , Aj , Uj ) from Ψmid independently. We
     discard the obtained Aj and instead let Aj be a sequence of uniformly sampled vectors
                                                          ′     E        E
                                         a(1) , . . . , a(q ) ∈ F2 j ⊗ F2 j .

      Let Ej′ = Ej , Bj = Aj and Uj′ = Uj .

  3. We use calligraphic fonts to denote a sequence of objects. For instance, write E = (E1 , . . . , Et)
                                                                                             E        E
     and U ′ = (U1′ , . . . , Ut′ ). Similarly define sequences A, B and U. Let V(E) = tj=1 F2 j ⊗ F2 j
                                                                                      L
                   L  E′                E′
                                            
     and V(E ′ ) = tj=1 F2 j ⊗ F2 j .

  4. Send (E, A, U) to Alice and (E ′ , B, U ′ ) to Bob.

  5. Alice must return a linear map f : V(E) → F2 such that for each j ∈ [t], the restriction of f
         E      E
     to F2 j ⊗ F2 j is symmetric and satisfies all equations in Ej , and rank(f |Uj ⊗Uj ) ⩽ 1. Bob must
     return a linear map g : V(E ′ ) → F2 .

  6. Accept if f |V(E ′ ) = g.

   Lazy repetition is easily seen to preserve perfect completeness. It also does not worsen the
soundness, because Alice and Bob still have to answer the questions (Ec , Ac , Uc ) and (Ec′ , Bc , Uc′ )
planted in the critical index, independent of what they do in the non-critical indices.
   The main benefit of the lazy repetition is that it improves the covering property. More precisely,
consider a random x ∈ V(E) obtained by the following process:

  1. Sample c and E ′ as in Ψmid .

                                                     17
   2. Sample y ∈ V(E ′ ), and lift to x ∈ V(E) by appending 0’s in missing coordinates (which only
      happens in the critical index).

Then, provided that t is sufficiently large compared to k, the distribution of x is close to uniform
in V(E). The intuition here comes from the following basic fact:

Claim 2.5. Suppose that (Ω, P) is a discrete probability space, and Γ ⊆ Ω√is an event such that
p = P(Γ) > 0. Then for each t, the following two distributions are Op (1/ t)-close in statistical
distance:

 (1) Sample x1 , . . . , xt ∼ P independently.

 (2) Sample c ∈ [t], sample xc ∼ P conditioned on xc ∈ Γ, and for j ̸= c sample xj ∼ P
     independently.

   In summary, the game Ψlazy has perfect completeness, small soundness, local tensor tests and
the covering property. It can actually be composed with an appropriate version of the Grassmann
graph but this is still not straightforward. In the next section we discuss why a naı̈ve composition
with the standard Grassmann encoding still fails, and explain how we circumvent this issue by
taking an appropriate variant of the Grassmann encoding.

2.2.6   Our Inner PCP
A natural attempt at composing Ψlazy with the Grassmann encoding is to encode the functions
f : V(E) → F2 and g : V(E ′ ) → F2 via the Grassmann encoding, and then check consistency via
the Grassmann test. While this test does satisfy the same notion of non-standard soundness as
discussed in Section 2.1.3, we do not know how to implement suitable advice vectors to facilitate the
zoom-in coordination. Indeed, the Grassmann encoding via Gr(V(E), ℓ1 ) would require uniformly
random advice vectors in V(E), and specifying a vector

would require |Ej |2 bits of information for each index j. However, although the advice A =

Composing with middle PCP: we can now make Ψlazy into a 4-to-1 game by using the low-

   2. For each i ∈ {1, 2, . . . , 2ℓ1 +1 − 1}, sample

      subspace of L′ with dimension ℓ1 . We regard L ⊆ V(E ′ ) as a subspace of V(E) by appending
      0’s in the missing coordinates (which only happens in the critical index).

  3
      Definition 2.6 will not be used explicitly in this paper.




                                                               19
                                                            
    5. Alice must return a linear map f : L + tj=1 Uj ⊗ Uj → F2 such that rank(f |Uj ⊗Uj ) ⩽ 1

       for each j ∈ [t]. Bob must return a linear map g : L′ + tj=1 Uj′ ⊗ Uj′ → F2 such that
                                                                 L

    6. Accept if the restriction of g to L + tj=1 Uj ⊗ Uj is identical to f .
                                                 L


    The game Ψfinal is our final composed PCP (modulo incorporating the side conditions checking
satisfiability of the chosen quadratic equations, and the side conditions checking symmetry; this is
done by adding the spaces NEj and Nsym as discussed in Section 2.2.1).
    It is easy to see that in Ψfinal , for any response of Alice, there are at most 4 possible answers of
Bob that can make the verifier accept. This is due to the following facts:

    • Since dim L′ = 1 + dim L, any linear function on L has two linear extensions to L′ .

    • For each j ∈ [t] \ {c}, we have Uj = Uj′ . For the critical index c, we have dim Uc′ = 1 + dim Uc ,
      and hence any symmetric bilinear function on Uc ⊗ Uc of rank at most 1 has two symmetric
      bilinear extensions to Uc′ ⊗ Uc′ of rank at most 1.

    The completeness of Ψfinal is clear by using the Grassmann encoding on the strategies of the
provers from Ψlazy . For soundness, we show that a strategy for Ψfinal achieving value at least ε can
be used to construct a strategy for Ψlazy achieving value at least Ωε (1). This argument is more
elaborate, and we only give a high-level overview.
    First, we describe a high-level overview where we pretend there was no need for zoom-outs
in Equation (2.2). We show that for noticeable fraction of the partial challenges (E, U) for Alice,
the table L 7→ F [E, U, L] passes the standard Grassmann test (Equation (2.1)) over V(E) with
noticeable probability. Applying Equation (2.2), we know that Alice can use the advice A in Ψlazy
as the zoom-in and thus decodes a global linear function g on V(E) which has rank 1 on Uj ⊗ Uj
for each j. We argue that for noticeable fraction of the challenges (E ′ , U ′ ) of Bob (conditioned on
E, U, A), the restriction g|V(E ′ ) is a global linear function for Bob that has noticeable agreement
with his table L′ 7→ G[E ′ , U ′ , L′ ] under the same zoom in (constructed from his advice). Together,
this shows that for a fixed successful advice, each one of Alice and Bob only has constantly many
global linear functions that have noticeable agreement with their table, and there is at least one
pair of choices between them which is consistent. Thus, if each one of them chooses a random such
global linear function, they will produce a satisfactory answer to Ψlazy with constant probability.
    The need for zoom-outs in Equation (2.2) complicates matters considerably, as the number of
“successful” zoom-outs could be rather large. This issue was already handled in [DKK+ 21], and
we will make use of some ideas from there. However, the fact that we use the low-rank Grassmann
encoding introduces further difficulties. In [DKK+ 21] it is shown that if Bob’s table has many
zoom-outs of codimension w that admit a global linear function with noticeable agreement, then
there is also a zoom-out of codimension w − 1 that admits a global linear function with noticeable
agreement. While this may be possible in our case, it requires extra features from the low-rank
Grassmann graph which we do not establish. Instead, we show how to stitch together global linear
functions from many different Bob-questions (E ′ , U ′ ) to a function of Alice, on which we can apply
the result of [DKK+ 21] (since Alice’s space has the standard Grassmann graph structure).


3     Preliminaries
In this section we set up basic notations and tools that will be used throughout the paper.

                                                   20
3.1   General Notations
In this subsection we summarize the general notational conventions used throughout the paper.
Additional notation will be introduced as needed, typically within dedicated “Notation” environ-
ments.

Linear algebra. Given a finite dimensional F2 -vector space V and a linear subspace W ⊆ V ,
the codimension of W with respect to V is denoted by codimV (W ) = dim V − dim W . Given a
finite dimensional F2 -vector space V , we let V ∗ denote the dual space of V , which consists of all
linear functionals f : V → F2 . For any finite-dimensional F2 -vector spaces V1 , V2 and any bilinear
form f : V1 ⊗ V2 → F2 , we define the linear map Tf : V1 → V2∗ by letting Tf (x) be the functional
y 7→ f (x ⊗ y).
    As indicated in Sections 2.2.4 and 2.2.5 of the proof overview, bilinear forms will play an
important role in our construction and analysis of 4-to-1 games. More linear algebraic preliminaries
about bilinear forms can be found in Appendix A.

Grassmann graph. Given a finite dimensional F2 -vector space V and a positive integer ℓ ⩽
dim V , the Grassmann graph Gr(V, ℓ) has as vertices all ℓ-dimensional subspaces L ⊆ V , and two
vertices are adjacent if they intersect in dimension ℓ − 1. By a slight abuse of notation, we also use
Gr(V, ℓ) to denote the vertex set of this graph. We denote by G(V, ℓ) the uniform distribution over
the edges of Gr(V, ℓ).

Probability. For a finite set Λ, we write Ex∈Λ [·] and Px∈Λ [·] to denote expectation and proba-
bility, respectively, when x is drawn uniformly at random from Λ. If x is sampled according to a
specific distribution D over Λ, we write x ∼ D in place of x ∈ Λ in the subscripts.

Assignment tables. In a 2-prover 1-round game, we often organize a prover’s strategy as an
assignment table F [·], where for each possible question Q from the verifier, the entry F [Q] takes
value from an alphabet of answers. It is often convenient to allow partial assignment tables, where
each entry F [Q] is allowed to take a special nullity value outside of the prescribed alphabet. We
usually denote such a nullity value by the symbol nil. Whenever a nil entry is tested for agreement
with other entries or objects, we always assume the test rejects (i.e. reports a disagreement). For
instance, if L1 and L2 are linear subspaces of a vector space V , the entry F [L1 ] takes value in
(L1 )∗ ∪ {nil} and F [L2 ] takes value in (L2 )∗ ∪ {nil}, then the statement
                                    h                                 i
                                      F [L1 ]|L1 ∩L2 = F [L2 ]|L1 ∩L2

evaluates to “false” whenever either of F [L1 ] or F [L2 ] is nil.

Empty sequences. A sequence of objects a(1) , . . . , a(q) is denoted by a(1) , . . . , a(q) . If q = 0,
                                                                                            

the sequence is empty and is denoted by the symbol empty.

3.2   Sub-4-to-1 Games
In order to prove the main theorem (Theorem 1.6), it is convenient to work with a slight relaxation
of the 4-to-1 requirement:




                                                   21
Definition 3.1. In the setting and notation of Definition 1.1, a constraint Φe ⊆ ΣL × ΣR is called a
sub-d-to-d′ constraint if there is a partition ΣL = ΣL,1 ⊔ · · · ⊔ ΣL,t into sets of size d and a partition
ΣR = ΣR,1 ⊔ · · · ⊔ ΣR,t into sets of size d′ such that
                         t
                         [
                  Φe =         ΓL,i × ΓR,i ,    for some subsets ΓL,i ⊆ ΣL,i and ΓR,i ⊆ ΣR,i .
                         i=1

      The notion of sub-d-to-d′ constraints motivates the following definition parallel to Definition 1.3.
Definition 3.2. Let d, d′ and r be positive integers. An instance Ψ of label-cover is called a
sub-d-to-d′ game if all of its constraints are sub-d-to-d′ . For parameters 0 < s < c ⩽ 1, we write
GapS-d-to-d′r (c, s) for the restriction of GapLCr (c, s) to sub-d-to-d′ game instances.
      Accordingly, we have the following slight weakening of Theorem 1.6.
Theorem 3.3. For any ε ∈ (0, 1), there exists a positive integer r such that GapS-4-to-1r (1, ε) is
NP-hard.
   Although Theorem 3.3 is a relaxation of Theorem 1.6, we will show in Appendix B that The-
orem 3.3 actually implies Theorem 1.6. The main body of this paper (Sections 4 to 6) will be
devoted to proving Theorem 3.3.

3.3      Technical Ingredients from Prior Work
In this subsection, we record the main technical ingredients of this paper that are imported from
prior works.

3.3.1      Approximate Coloring of Hypergraphs
As discussed in Section 2.2.4, our construction starts from the hardness of approximating satisfiable
3QSF2 instances. However, limitations of the tensor tests used in our middle PCP require us to start
from an NP-hard gap problem in which the “yes” and “no” cases impose conditions on different
constraint satisfaction instances. Specifically, the soundness condition in Theorem 2.4 must assert
that, even after the alphabet of the 3QSF2 instance is suitably enlarged (from F2 to (F2 )r , roughly
speaking), the resulting 3QS(F2 )r instance still has small value.4
    Fortunately, the desired starting point in Theorem 2.4 is essentially implicit in the result
of [DRS05] on the hardness of coloring 2-colorable 3-uniform hypergraphs.5 Although their result
is stated with the no-case condition that the hypergraph H is not c-colorable, a closer inspection
of their argument yields the statement below.
Theorem 3.4 ([DRS05]). For every fixed positive integer c ⩾ 2, there exists a constant
ε ⩾ exp(− exp(O(c3 ))) such that given a 3-uniform hypergraph H, it is NP-hard to distinguish
between the following cases:
 (1) Yes case: H is 2-colorable.
 (2) No case: in every c-coloring of H, at least ε fraction of the edges are monochromatic.
   Since Theorem 3.4 differs slightly from the result proved in [DRS05], we provide a proof in Ap-
pendix C. Our proof is a minor adaptation of their argument, which in turn uses the PCP theo-
rem [FGL+ 96, AS98, ALM+ 98] and parallel repetition [Raz98].
  4
      This formulation is characteristic of promise constraint satisfaction problems (PCSPs); see, for example, [KO22].
  5
      In Section 4.2, we will explain why 2-coloring a 3-uniform hypergraph can be viewed as solving a 3QSF2 instance.


                                                           22
3.3.2   Grassmann Agreement Test
The main analytical/combinatorial tool used in this paper is the (non-standard) soundness of the
Grassmann agreement test, informally stated in Equation (2.2). Under the notation specified
in Section 3.1, given a table F [·] that assigns to each ℓ-dimensional subspace L ⊆ Fn2 either a linear
function F [L] : L → F2 or the nullity element nil, the passing probability of the Grassmann test is
                                             h                                i
                                    P n        F [L1 ]|L1 ∩L2 = F [L2 ]|L1 ∩L2 ,
                                (L1 ,L2 )∼G(F2 ,ℓ)

where the event does not hold if either F [L1 ] or F [L2 ] are nil. The following result is due
to [KMS23, EKL23], but we use a slightly different formulation which we derive directly from
their results.

Theorem 3.5 ([KMS23, EKL23]). There is an absolute constant C > 0 such that the following
holds. Let n, ℓ, q be nonnegative integers such that n − q − 3 ⩾ ℓ ⩾ q + 3. Suppose F [·] is a table
where F [L] ∈ L∗ ∪ {nil} for each L ∈ Gr(Fn2 , ℓ), such that
                                      h                                i
                             P n        F [L1 ]|L1 ∩L2 = F [L2 ]|L1 ∩L2 ⩾ 2−q+5 .              (3.1)
                       (L1 ,L2 )∼G(F2 ,ℓ)

                       2
Then for at least 2−q−ℓ fraction of subspaces Q ∈ Gr(Fn2 , q), there exist a subspace W ∈ Gr(Fn2 , n−q)
containing Q and a linear map f : W → F2 such that
                                     h                           i         2
                               Pn      F [L] = f |L Q ⊆ L ⊆ W ⩾ 2−Cq .
                               L∈Gr(F2 ,ℓ)

    A derivation of Theorem 3.5 from the formulation of [EKL23] will be presented in Appendix D.
    We also need the following simpler result, which concerns not a table of local linear functions,
but a table where every entry takes value from the same abstract alphabet. The following result
asserts that if the “Grassmann equality test” passes with noticeable probability, then after zoom-ins
and zoom-outs the entries take a certain constant value noticeably often.

Theorem 3.6. There is an absolute constant C > 0 such that the following holds. Let n, ℓ, q be
nonnegative integers such that n − q − 3 ⩾ ℓ ⩾ q + 3. Suppose Σ is a finite set and F [·] is a table
where F [L] ∈ Σ ∪ {nil} for each L ∈ Gr(Fn2 , ℓ), such that
                                       h                        i
                                P n     F [L1 ] = F [L2 ] ̸= nil ⩾ 2−q+4 .                     (3.2)
                               (L1 ,L2 )∼G(F2 ,ℓ)

                           2
Then for at least 2−q−ℓ fraction of subspaces Q ∈ Gr(Fn2 , q), there exist an element σ ∈ Σ and a
subspace W ∈ Gr(Fn2 , n − q) containing Q such that
                                     h                       i        2
                                Pn    F [L] = σ Q ⊆ L ⊆ W ⩾ 2−Cq .
                                L∈Gr(F2 ,ℓ)

Proof. Deferred to Appendix D.

3.3.3   List Decoding Bounds
When we use Theorem 3.5 we will often need to argue that given a zoom-in Q, the number of
possible decodings (f, W ) is not too large. In this section we precisely define this notion and state
the bounds we will use, established by [DKK+ 25].

                                                     23
Definition 3.7 ([DKK+ 25]). Let n, ℓ, q, w be nonnegative integers such that n − w ⩾ ℓ ⩾ q + 1.
Fix parameters τ0 , τ1 , . . . , τw ∈ (0, 1) and write ⃗τ = (τ0 , . . . , τw ). Fix an F2 -vector space V of
dimension n and a subspace Q ∈ Gr(V, q). Suppose F [·] is a table where F [L] ∈ L∗ ∪ {nil} for each
L ∈ Gr(V, ℓ). For a linear subspace W ⊆ V with codim W ⩽ w and a linear functional f : W → F2 ,
we say that the pair (f, W ) is ⃗τ -occurring in F [·] modulo Q if Q ⊆ W and
                                 h                   i
                       P           F [L] = f |L Q ⊆ L ⩾ τi ,     where i = n − dim W.
                   L∈Gr(W,ℓ)

    It is not possible to give a bound on the number of ⃗τ -occurring pairs that is independent of n,
simply because the number of zoom-outs W can be very large. We have to restrict our attention
to pairs (f, W ) that “cannot be extended,” as defined below.

Definition 3.8. In the setting and notation of Definition 3.7, we say that (f, W ) is ⃗τ -maximal in
F [·] modulo Q if

 (1) (f, W ) is ⃗τ -occurring in F [·] modulo Q, but

 (2) for any linear subspace W ′ ⊆ V that properly contains W and any extended linear functional
     f ′ : W ′ → F2 , f ′ |W = f , the pair (f ′ , W ′ ) is not ⃗τ -occurring in F [·] modulo Q.

The set of all ⃗τ -maximal pairs will be denoted by LIST⃗τQ (F ).

   We use the following bound on the size of maximal pairs proved by [DKK+ 25].

Lemma 3.9 ([DKK+ 25]). There is an absolute constant C ⩾ 40 such that the following holds. Let
n, ℓ, q, w be nonnegative integers such that n − w ⩾ 2ℓ ⩾ C(q + 1). Suppose ⃗τ = (τ0 , . . . , τw ) is a
vector such that
                       τ0 ⩾ 2−ℓ/C      and     τi−1 ⩽ C −1 τiC for each i ∈ [w].
Then for any table F [·] where F [L] ∈ L∗ ∪ {nil} for each L ∈ Gr(Fn2 , ℓ), we have

                                     LIST⃗τQ (F ) ⩽ exp(C(w + 1)2 ℓ).

    In a part of the analysis of our inner PCP, we are not able to use Lemma 3.9 as a black box, and
we will instead draw from the proof of Lemma 3.9. A useful component of the proof by [DKK+ 25]
is the notion of sunflower of zoom-outs, defined as follows.

Definition 3.10. Let V be a vector space over F2 , and let W1 , . . . , Wm ⊆ V be subspaces of
co-dimension w. We say that (V, W1 , . . . , Wm ) forms a sunflower if codimV (Wi ∩ Wj ) = 2w for each
pair of distinct indices i, j ∈ [m].

    In words, a sunflower is a collection of subspaces W1 , . . . , Wm living inside a space V that
have as small as possible pairwise intersections. The following result from [DKK+ 25] asserts that
any sufficiently large collection of zoom-outs of constant codimension contains a large sunflower
structure.

Lemma 3.11 ([DKK+ 25, Corollary 3.14]). Let V be a finite dimensional F2 -vector space, and
let W1 , . . . , WN ⊆ V be a list of subspaces of codimension w. Let m be a positive integer such
that m · (m · 2w )w ⩽ N . Then, there exists a subspace W ⊆ V containing m of the Wi ’s, say,
W1 , . . . , Wm , such that (W, W1 , . . . , Wm ) forms a sunflower.



                                                    24
3.4    Proof Organization
The proof of Theorem 3.3 spans Sections 4 to 6, during which we construct a sequence of five
2-prover 1-round games; see Figure 4.


        Game1a             Game1b                 Game2a              Game2b                  Game3
      (Section 4.3)      (Section 4.4)          (Section 5.2)       (Section 5.4)          (Section 6.3)

               Outer PCP                                   Middle PCP                      Inner PCP

                                       Figure 4: Proof organization

   For each version label xx ∈ {1a, 1b, 2a, 2b, 3}, we will prove completeness and soundness guar-
antees of the following form:

 (1) Completeness: if H is 2-colorable, then val(Gamexx[H]) = 1.

 (2) Soundness: if every c-coloring of H makes at least ε fraction of the edges monochromatic,
     then val(Gamexx[H]) is small.

    Completeness is straightforward and can be proved independently for each game, without invok-
ing the completeness of preceding games. Most of the work lies in establishing soundness: for each
game after the first, the soundness proof relies on that of its predecessor in the sequence. The final
construction Game3[H] is a sub-4-to-1 game, whose completeness and soundness yield Theorem 3.3.

3.5    Parameter Relations
During the course of our construction of 4-to-1 games, there will be many parameters on different
scales (they are all constant with respect to the size of the starting hardness instance in Theo-
rem 3.4). We summarize the relations between these constants in the following condition.

Condition 3.12. Let k, q0 , q1 , q2 , t, r, ℓ1 , ℓ2 , s be positive integers, let δ, β, ε ∈ (0, 1), and let ⃗τ =
(τ0 , τ1 , . . . , τ2q0 ) be a vector such that for a sufficiently large absolute constant C > 0, the conditions
in Table 1 hold.
