4     The Outer PCP
In this section, we formally construct and analyze our outer PCP, following the outline in Sec-
tion 2.2.4. We first introduce the necessary framework in Sections 4.1 and 4.2 before turning to the
actual construction in Sections 4.3 and 4.4.

4.1     Preparation: Tripled Sets
As discussed in Sections 2.1.1 and 2.2.4, Alice’s challenge in the outer PCP consists of a set E of
variables together with an advice sequence. The variables in E are naturally grouped into triples,
each consisting of the three distinct variables appearing in an equation of the 3QSF2 instance. We
call such a set E a tripled set, formally defined as follows.



                                                                26
Definition 4.1. A tripled set is a finite set E equipped with a partition into subsets of size 3.
We refer to the parts of this implicit partition as the triples of E, and we write Triples(E) for the
collection of its triples.
   As discussed in Sections 2.1.1 and 2.2.4, our outer PCP construction applies (smooth) parallel
repetition to a 3QSF2 instance. Since the 3QSF2 instance provided by Theorem 3.4 is represented
by a 3-uniform hypergraph H, k-fold parallel repetition yields a 2-prover 1-round game played on
the vertex-disjoint union of k copies of H.
Definition 4.2. Let H be a 3-uniform hypergraph with vertex set V (H) and edge set E (H). Given
a positive integer k, we let H ⊔k denote the k-fold vertex-disjoint union of H. More explicitly, the
vertex
Sk      set of H ⊔k is [k] × V (H), and we denote V (H, k) = [k] × V (H); the edge set of H ⊔k is
  i=1 Ei (H, k), where

                       Ei (H, k) = {(i, v1 ), (i, v2 ), (i, v3 )} {v1 , v2 , v3 } ∈ E (H) .
                                  

We let E (H, k) be the collection of all vertex subsets E ⊆ [k] × V (H) that result from picking one
edge from each Ei (H, k). That is, it consists of E of the form

     E = {vi,j | i ∈ [k], j ∈ [3]}      such that  {vi,1 , vi,2 , vi,3 } ∈ Ei (H, k) for all i ∈ [k],   (4.1)
                                                                   
and we view it naturally as a tripled set, with Triples(E) = {vi,1 , vi,2 , vi,3 } i ∈ [k] .
    In words, viewing the set of hyperedges in H as the challenges that a verifier samples, the edges
in H ⊔k are naturally viewed as the challenges that the verifier chooses in the k-fold repeated game.
Remark 4.3. For a tripled set E of the form (4.1), the k triples of E are naturally ordered. For
each i ∈ [k], we will call {vi,1 , vi,2 , vi,3 } the i-th triple of E.

4.1.1    Thinning, and Thinning with Advice
We next introduce notation to formalize the smoothness in our application of parallel repetition.
The following definition captures the structure of the challenges in each coordinate: with probability
1 − β, the two players receive identical challenges; otherwise, Alice receives the full challenge, while
Bob receives only a single variable from it.
Definition 4.4. Let E be a tripled set and let β ∈ [0, 1]. We write E ′ ∼ Thinβ (E) for the random
subset E ′ ⊆ E obtained as follows. Independently for each triple T ∈ Triples(E), we include all
elements of T in E ′ with probability 1 − β, and include a uniformly random element of T in E ′
with probability β. We stress that the output E ′ itself is only a set, not a tripled set.
    In the outer PCP construction, each prover will receive two advice sequences Ain and Amid ,
the latter supplying the Grassmann-type zoom-ins needed in the middle PCP, while the former is
reserved for zoom-ins in the inner PCP. The next definition formalizes the process by which the
verifier samples the two advice sequences.
Definition 4.5. Let E be a tripled set. Fix nonnegative integers q1 , q2 and a parameter β ∈ [0, 1].
We let Jout (E, q1 , q2 , β) denote the output distribution of the following process:
   1. Sample a β-thinning E ′ ∼ Thinβ (E).
                                                           ′
   2. Sample q = q1 + q2 vectors a(1) , . . . , a(q) ∈ FE
                                                         2 independently and uniformly at random. We
      denote Ain = a(1) , . . . , a(q1 ) and Amid = a(q1 +1) , . . . , a(q1 +q2 ) .

                                                      27
  3. Output the tuple (E ′ , Ain , Amid ).
                                                                                 ′
Remark 4.6. For a subset E ′ of a finite set E, we always view FE          2 as a linear subspace of the
ambient space FE 2 . In particular, the vectors a (1) , . . . , a(q) in Definition 4.5 are viewed also as

elements of the vector space FE
                              2.

   In the outer PCP, Alice will see a tripled set E and the two advice sequences Ain and Amid , but
not the β-thinning E ′ which is only sent to Bob. The next proposition states that, even conditioned
on Ain and Amid , the distribution of E ′ is still nicely structured. In particular, it is independent
over the triples of E.

Proposition 4.7. Let E be a tripled set. Fix nonnegative integers q1 , q2 and a parameter β ∈ [0, 1).

Proof. Deferred to Appendix F.1.

    As emphasized throughout the proof overview, “covering properties” are crucial in our construc-
tion of 4-to-1 games, and they will appear many times (in different forms) throughout Sections 4

4.2   Preparation: The NAE Predicate
The reason that 2-colorability of a 3-uniform hypergraph can be viewed as a system of quadratic
equations is that the ternary not-all-equal predicate on a Boolean alphabet is a “quadratic” con-
straint:



                                                       28
Definition 4.9. We define a function NAE3 : F32 → F2 defined by

                         NAE3 (x1 , x2 , x3 ) = x1 + x2 + x3 + x1 x2 + x2 x3 + x3 x1 .

We note that NAE3 (x1 , x2 , x3 ) = 1 if and only if x1 , x2 , x3 are not all equal.

   As discussed in Section 2.2.4, we will use the notion of “joint NAE-satisfaction”, which we
formalize below. Here we assume that an assignment to the variables in E is presented as a linear
functional f : FE
                2 → F2 .

then the linear functionals f (1) , . . . , f (r) are said to be jointly NAE-satisfying. In the case r = 1,
then the functional f (1) is simply said to be NAE-satisfying.

    We can now translate the “2-colorability” of a 3-uniform hypergraph H into the language of
NAE-satisfaction. For the sake of future reference, the following proposition is stated for the k-fold
disjoint union H ⊔k .

Proposition 4.11. Suppose H is a 2-colorable 3-uniform hypergraph and k is a positive integer.

4.3      The Equation vs. Variable Game
We are now ready to start the construction of our outer PCP. The first version of our outer PCP
is defined as follows (cf. Section 2.1.1).

Definition 4.18. Given positive integers k, r, nonnegative integers q1 , q2 , a parameter β ∈ [0, 1]
and a 3-uniform hypergraph H, let Game1a[H, k, q1 , q2 , r, β] be the 2-prover 1-round game where
the verifier executes the following:

   1. Sample a uniformly random tripled set E ∈ E (H, k).

   2. Sample (E ′ , Ain , Amid ) ∼ Jout (E, q1 , q2 , β). Send (E, Ain , Amid ) to Alice6 and (E ′ , Ain , Amid )
      to Bob.

   3. Alice must return r linear functionals f (1) , . . . , f (r) : FE
                                                                      2 ′ → F2 that are jointly NAE-satisfying;
      Bob must return r linear functionals g (1) , . . . , g (r) : FE 2 → F2 . Accept if


                                            f (i)        ′
                                                             = g (i)       for all i ∈ [r].
                                                    FE
                                                     2
                                                                                          ′
   6
    In Alice’s view, Ain and Amid are sequences of vectors in FE                  E
                                                               2 , instead of in F2 ; it is important that Alice does not
            ′
get to see E .




                                                                 31
Remark 4.19. We remark that the game Game1a[H, k, q1 , q2 , r, β] can be viewed as the k-fold
parallel repetition of Game1a[H, 1, q1 , q2 , r, β], and the game Game1a[H, 1, 0, 0, r, 1] is the equation-
vs.-variable game corresponding to the result of Theorem 3.4.

   We now analyze the completeness and the soundness of Game1a.

Lemma 4.20 (Game1a completeness). For any positive integer k, nonnegative integers q1 , q2 ,
parameter β ∈ [0, 1] and 2-colorable 3-uniform hypergraph H, we have
                                                               
                                val Game1a[H, k, q1 , q2 , 1, β] = 1.

                                                                                V (H,k)
Proof. We obtain from Proposition 4.11 a linear functional f : F2     → F2 , whose restriction on
F2 is NAE-satisfying for any E ∈ E (H, k). We can then construct a strategy for Alice in the game
 E

Game1a[H, k, q1 , q2 , 1, β], as follows:

   1. Receive the question (E, Ain , Amid ).

   2. Return the functional f |FE , which is guaranteed by Proposition 4.11 to be NAE-satisfying.
                                   2

Similarly, the strategy for Bob is:

   1. Receive the question (E ′ , Ain , Amid ).

   2. Return the functional f |FE′ .
                                   2

It is clear that Bob’s answer is always consistent with Alice’s answer. Therefore, if Alice and
Bob operate under such strategies, the verifier in the game Game1a[H, k, q1 , q2 , 1, β] accepts with
probability 1.

   To show soundness of Game1a[H, k, q1 , q2 , r, β], we start by analyzing the soundness of the basic
game Game1a[H, 1, 0, 0, r, 1], which is effectively the standard equation-versus-variable game with
“superpositions.”

Lemma 4.21. Fix a positive integer r and a parameter ε ∈ (0, 1). Suppose H is a 3-uniform
hypergraph such that in every 2r -coloring of H, at least ε fraction of the edges are monochromatic.
Then we have                                                      ε
                               val Game1a[H, 1, 0, 0, r, 1] ⩽ 1 − .

   Next, we incorporate the advice as well as constantly many repetitions, and show that the value
does not increase by too much:

Lemma 4.22. Fix positive integers k, r, nonnegative integers q1 , q2 and parameters β, ε ∈ (0, 1)
such that k ⩾ 2q1 +q2 β −1 . Suppose H is a 3-uniform hypergraph such that in every 2r -coloring of

                                                               33
   Finally, we apply the parallel repetition theorem for projection games by [Rao11] to get the
soundness of Game1a.

Lemma 4.23 (Game1a soundness). Assume Condition 3.12. Suppose H is a 3-uniform hypergraph
such that in every 4r -coloring of H, at least ε fraction of the edges are monochromatic. Then we
have7                                                       

                                                                       ε
                                val Game1a[H, k0 , q1 , q2 , 2r, β] ⩽ 1 − .
                                                                         6
Since Game1a[H, k0 , q1 , q2 , 2r, β] is a projection game, we may apply [Rao11, Theorem 1.4] to con-
clude that its ⌊k/k0 ⌋-fold parallel repetition has value at most exp(−cε2 ⌊k/k0 ⌋), for some absolute
constant c ∈ (0, 1). It is then easy to see (via Remark 4.19) that
                                                   
                 val Game1a[H, k, q1 , q2 , 2r, β] ⩽ exp(−cε2 ⌊k/k0 ⌋) ⩽ exp(−k 1/5 ),

where in the last transition we use the relation k/k0 ≫ ε−2 k 1/5 guaranteed by Condition 3.12.

4.4      From Joint Satisfaction to Bilinear Forms
As hinted in Section 2.2.4 and Section 4.2.1, the last goal in the outer PCP stage is to change the
format of the provers’ responses to symmetric bilinear forms. More precisely, we will consider the
following 2-prover 1-round game defined below. It is identical to Game1a[H, k, q1 , q2 , r, β], except
that the provers are expected to provide a single bilinear form of low-rank (instead of r linear
forms).

Definition 4.24. Given positive integers k, r, nonnegative integers q1 , q2 , a parameter β ∈ [0, 1)
and a 3-uniform hypergraph H, let Game1b[H, k, q1 , q2 , r, β] be the 2-prover-1-round game where
the verifier executes the following:

   1. Sample a uniformly random tripled set E ∈ E (H, k).

   2. Sample (E ′ , Ain , Amid ) ∼ Jout (E, q1 , q2 , β). Send (E, Ain , Amid ) to Alice and (E ′ , Ain , Amid ) to
      Bob.

   3. Alice must return a symmetric bilinear form f : FE      E
                                                        2 ⊗ F2 →   F2 that is NAE-satisfying and
                                                                 ′    ′
      has rank at most r; Bob must return a bilinear form g : F2 ⊗ FE
                                                               E
                                                                     2 → F2 . Accept if

                                                      f |FE′ ⊗FE′ = g.
                                                         2    2



      We now analyze the completeness and the soundness of Game1b.

Lemma 4.25 (Game1b completeness). For any positive integer k, nonnegative integers q1 , q2 ,
parameter β ∈ [0, 1) and 2-colorable 3-uniform hypergraph H, we have
                                                               

It is clear that under this set of strategies, the verifier accepts with probability 1.

   We now move on to the soundness. The following lemma establishes a connection between the
soundness of Game1a and Game1b, and more precisely it shows how to derive a strategy for the
former from a strategy for the latter. Intuitively, this comes from the fact that a rank r bilinear
form has at most Or (1) many decompositions into rank-1 bilinear forms, but some care is needed
(specifically, the event that the rank of Alice’s bilinear form drops in Bob’s space is problematic,
but thankfully it has low probability).

and 3-uniform hypergraph H, we have

Proof. We organize the proof into the following three steps.

Step 1: controlling rank drop.            We denote
                                                                        

5     The Middle PCP
In this section we construct and analyze our middle PCP as discussed in Section 2.2.5. Sections 5.1
to 5.3 are devoted to the design and analysis of Game2a, where we incorporate local tensor checks.
We then perform a lazy parallel repetition to get Game2b in Section 5.4.

5.1     Preparation: Thinning with Subspaces
In the middle PCP, we will use a Grassmann-type structure to perform “local tensor checks.” Along
with a question (E, Ain , Amid ) sent to Alice (as in the outer PCP), the verifier also sends a subspace
U ⊆ FE                                                                E     E
       2 and checks whether the restriction of Alice’s answer f : F2 ⊗ F2 → F2 to U ⊗ U has rank
at most 1. The following augmentation of Definition 4.5 captures the process by which the verifier
samples the tensor-check subspace U .

Definition 5.1. Let E be a tripled set. Fix a parameter β ∈ [0, 1] and nonnegative integers q1 , q2 , ℓ
such that ℓ ⩾ q2 . We let Jmid (E, q1 , q2 , β, ℓ) denote the output distribution of the following process:

                                                       38
5.2    The Local Tensor Check Game
We now construct the first version of our middle PCP. The 2-prover 1-round game constructed in
the next definition is similar to Game1b but with one important distinction. Letting f be Alice’s
response, instead of requiring rank(f ) is small, we require that rank(f |U ⊗U ) is small, where U is
(roughly) a random ℓ-dimensional subspace in Alice’s space FE 2 which is given to Alice as part of her
challenge. Additionally, as part of his challenge, we give Bob a random subspace U ′ of dimension
(roughly) ℓ + 1 that contains U .

Definition 5.7. Given positive integers k, ℓ2 , nonnegative integers q1 , q2 such that ℓ2 ⩾ q2 , a
parameter β ∈ [0, 1) and a 3-uniform hypergraph H, let Game2a[H, k, ℓ2 , q1 , q2 , β] be the 2-Prover-
1-Round game where the verifier executes the following:

   1. Sample a uniformly random tripled set E ∈ E (H, k).
                                                                                                     ′
   2. Sample (E ′ , Ain , Amid , U ) ∼ Jmid (E, q1 , q2 , β, ℓ2 ). Sample a random vector x ∈ FE
                                                                                               2 and let
      U ′ = U + span(x).

   3. Send (E, Ain , U ) to Alice and (E ′ , Ain , U ′ ) to Bob.

   4. Alice must return an NAE-satisfying symmetric bilinear form f : FE       E
                                                                         2 ⊗ F2 → F2 such that
                                                               E ′  E ′
      rank(f |U ⊗U ) ⩽ 1; Bob must return a bilinear form g : F2 ⊗ F2 → F2 . Accept if

                                                   f |FE′ ⊗FE′ = g.
                                                       2    2


Remark 5.8. In Definition 5.7, the verifier does not use Amid after generating it. Therefore,
the distribution of (E, Ain , U, U ′ ) does not depend on q2 . Consequently, the value of the game
Game2a[H, k, ℓ2 , q1 , q2 , β] does not depend on q2 either. The purpose of Amid in Definition 5.7 is
solely for the convenience of its soundness analysis in Section 5.3.

Remark 5.9. In Definition 5.7, it is easy to see that the distribution of (E ′ , Ain , Amid , U ′ ) is identical
to Jmid (E, q1 , q2 , β, ℓ2 + 1).

    The completeness of Game2a is straightforward and is given in the following lemma.

Lemma 5.10 (Game2a completeness). For any positive integers k, ℓ2 , nonnegative integers q1 , q2
such that k ⩾ ℓ2 ⩾ q2 , parameter β ∈ [0, 1) and 2-colorable 3-uniform hypergraph H, we have
                                                                 
                                val Game2a[H, k, ℓ2 , q1 , q2 , β] = 1.

Proof. It is clear that the provers can ignore the subspaces U or U ′ given to them and pretend that
they are in the game Game1b[H, k, q1 , 0, 1, β]. Since there is a strategy for Game1b[H, k, q1 , 0, 1, β]
with 0 rejection probability due to Lemma 4.25, it follows that the same strategy also passes
Game2a[H, k, ℓ2 , q1 , q2 , β] with probability 1.

    The soundness analysis of Game2a, presented in the next subsection, is much more involved.
Intuitively, if we did not supply Bob with the space U ′ , the analysis would be relatively straight-
forward. Indeed, in that case, while in principle Alice’s response could depend on the space U she
receives, in actuality such strategies do not achieve a high value, as they have to be consistent with
Bob (whose response does not depend on U at all). Thus, in that case Alice’s response can be
thought of as not being dependent on U , and hence the tensor test on U is random and faithfully
checks whether Alice’s response has low-rank globally.

                                                       39
   To make our final game 4-to-1 we must supply Bob with the space U ′ (otherwise we would not
be able to compose with the inner PCP), and the soundness analysis in that case becomes more
complicated. Indeed, there are genuine cheating strategies that Alice and Bob can use, and our
argument below analyzes them (using the soundness of the Grassmann test) and shows that they
can still be converted into good strategies for Game1b.

5.3    Soundness Analysis
In this subsection, we fix a 3-uniform hypergraph H, positive integers k, ℓ2 , nonnegative integers
q1 , q2 and a parameter β ∈ (0, 1). We consider the game Game2a[H, k, ℓ2 , q1 , q2 , β] as defined
in Definition 5.7. The following notational convention will be used throughout Section 5.3 (but not
in later parts of the paper).
Notation 5.11. We use Dmid to denote the joint distribution of (E, Ain , Amid , U, E ′ , U ′ ) in the
game Game2a[H, k, ℓ2 , q1 , q2 , β], where (E, Ain , U ) is the question sent to Alice and (E ′ , Ain , U ′ ) is
the question sent to Bob. We use the symbol ∗ to denote marginalization over the corresponding
component. For instance, sampling from the marginal distribution of (Ain , U, E ′ ) under Dmid is
denoted by (∗, Ain , ∗, U, E ′ , ∗) ∼ Dmid .
Notation 5.12. We will often condition the distribution Dmid on some subset of the six components
(E, Ain , Amid , U, E ′ , U ′ ) and consider the induced joint distribution of the remaining components.
For instance, if E and U ′ are fixed, then the conditional joint distribution of (U, E ′ ) is denoted by

                                                              · E, U ′ .

Notation 5.13. A table F [·] is called an Alice-table if for each possible Alice-question (E, Ain , U )
under Dmid , the entry F [E, Ain , U ] is either nil or an NAE-satisfying symmetric bilinear form

          F [E, Ain , U ] : FE     E

                             2 ⊗ F2 → F2         such that   rank F [E, Ain , U ]|U ⊗U ⩽ 1.

Similarly, a table G[·] is called a Bob-table if for each possible Bob-question (E ′ , Ain , U ′ ) under

                                                                   ′   ′
                                       G[E ′ , Ain , U ′ ] : FE    E

   In the following notation, the function Vrf F,G (·, ·, ·, ·, ·) is the indicator of whether the verifier
accepts under the strategy given by F [·] and G[·]. Since Amid is irrelevant to the verifier’s acceptance,
the Vrf F,G (·, ·, ·, ·, ·) only has five arguments instead of six.

with nonzero probability under Dmid , we define

When none of the components is fixed, we write Vrf F,G = Vrf F,G (∗, ∗, ∗, ∗, ∗) for short.

5.3.3   Deriving Strategy for the Outer PCP
We now use the structural results regarding Alice’s strategy to complete the soundness analysis of
Game2a. Before we do so, fixing an Alice-table and a Bob-table, we prune the Alice-table and only
keep the responses on questions which have noticeable success probability. This will ensure that
whatever functions we decode from Alice’s table have noticeable consistency with Bob’s table.

Definition 5.17. Let η ∈ (0, 1) be a threshold parameter. Given an Alice-table F [·] and a Bob-
table G[·] for Game2a[H, k, ℓ2 , q1 , q2 , β], consider the Alice-table F ′ [·] defined by
                                       (
                    ′                     F [E, Ain , U ], if Vrf F,G (E, Ain , U, ∗, ∗) ⩾ η,
                  F [E, Ain , U ] =
                                          nil,             otherwise.

The pair of tables (F ′ , G) is called the η-pruning of (F, G).

    The following straightforward proposition shows that η-pruning drops the success probability
of Alice and Bob by at most η.

                                                        44
Proposition 5.18. Let η ∈ (0, 1) be a threshold parameter. Given an Alice-table F [·] and a
Bob-table G[·] for Game2a[H, k, ℓ2 , q1 , q2 , β], let (F ′ , G) be the η-pruning of (F, G). Then

                                                 Vrf F ′ ,G ⩾ Vrf F,G −η.

Proof. For any E ∈ E (H, k), any Ain ∈ (FE     2)
                                                   q1 and any U ∈ Gr(FE , ℓ ), from Definition 5.17 it is
                                                                            2   2
easy to see that
                      Vrf F ′ ,G (E, Ain , U, ∗, ∗) ⩾ Vrf F,G (E, Ain , U, ∗, ∗) − η.
Taking expectation over (E, Ain , ∗, U, ∗, ∗) ∼ Dmid then yields the desired conclusion.

   We are now ready to state and prove the soundness of Game2a.

Lemma 5.19 (Game2a soundness). Assume Condition 3.12. Suppose that in every 4r -coloring of

where we used Condition 3.12 in the last transition. Since the distribution of (E, Ain , Amid , E ′ ) in
Game1b[H, k, q1 , q2 , r, β] is identical to the distribution of the sample (E, Ain , Amid , ∗, E ′ , ∗) ∼ Dmid ,
it follows that our prescribed strategies pass Game1b[H, k, q1 , q2 , r, β] with probability
                                                                  
                                   val Game1b[H, k, q1 , q2 , r, β] > k −1/2 ,
contradicting Lemma 4.27.
   9
   Here we use the fact that Vrf F ′ ,G (E, Ain , ∗, ∗, ∗) always lies in [0, 1].
   Here we use Alice(E, Ain , Amid ) to denote Alice’s answer to the question (E, Ain , Amid ), and Bob(E ′ , Ain , Amid )
  10

denotes Bob’s answer to (E ′ , Ain , Amid ).


                                                                         46
5.4   Lazy Parallel Repetition
In this subsection, we apply a “lazy parallel repetition” to Game2a to get Game2b. More precisely,
we consider the following 2-prover 1-round game, in which Alice and Bob get t-blocks of challenges,
one of which is a true challenge from Game2a, whereas on each one of the rest of the blocks they
receive the same randomly sampled challenge of Alice from Game2a. Additionally, on each block
the players receive a list of advice vectors.
Definition 5.20. Given positive integers k, ℓ2 , t, nonnegative integers q0 , q1 , a parameter β ∈ [0, 1)
and a 3-uniform hypergraph H, let Game2b[H, k, ℓ2 , t, q0 , q1 , β] be a 2-Prover 1-Round game where
the verifier executes the following:
  1. For each j ∈ [t], sample a uniformly random tripled set Ej ∈ E (H, k).

  2. Sample a uniformly random index c ∈ [t]. Sample (Ec′ , Ac , empty, Uc ) ∼ Jmid (Ec , q1 , 0, β, ℓ2 ).
                                 E′
     Sample a random vector x ∈ F2 c and let Uc′ = Uc + span(x).

  3. For each j ∈ [t] \ {c}, do the following:
                                                                                       E      E q
         • Sample a uniformly random advice sequence Aj = a(j,1) , . . . , a(j,q0 ) ∈ F2 j ⊗ F2 j 0 ,
                                                                                   
                                                                                  E       E
           i.e. a(j,i) is a uniformly random vector in F2 j ⊗ F2 j for each i ∈ [q0 ].
         • Sample (Ejghost , empty, empty, Uj ) ∼ Jmid (Ej , 0, 0, β, ℓ2 ).
         • Let Ej′ = Ej and Uj′ = Uj .

  4. Denote sequences

                  E = (E1 , . . . , Et ),                U = (U1 , . . . , Ut ),           A = (A1 , . . . , At ),   and
                      ′
                  E       = (E1′ , . . . , Et′ ),    U    ′
                                                              = (U1′ , . . . , Ut′ ).

  5. Send (E, A, U) to Alice and (c, E ′ , A, U ′ ) to Bob.

  6. Alice must return NAE-satisfying symmetric bilinear forms
                              E         E
                  fj : F2 j ⊗ F2 j → F2 such that rank(fj |Uj ⊗Uj ) ⩽ 1                                 for each j ∈ [t];

      Bob must return bilinear forms
                                                    E′           E′
                                             gj : F2 j ⊗ F2 j → F2                    for each j ∈ [t].
                                                                   E′        E′
      Accept if gj is the restriction of fj to F2 j ⊗ F2 j for each j ∈ [t].
   If we ignore the lists Aj , the game we have above is exactly the lazy parallel repetition version
of Game2a. Lazy parallel repetition is well-known to preserve completeness and soundness (but
not improve them), as well as to improve the “smoothness” of a game. We include the lists Aj to
enable the advice feature in the inner PCP, and we show that they do not hurt the soundness of
the construction (they have no effect on other features of the construction).
   The completeness of Game2b is again straightforward:
Lemma 5.21 (Game2b completeness). For any positive integers k, ℓ2 , t such that k ⩾ ℓ2 , nonneg-
ative integers q0 , q1 , parameter β ∈ [0, 1) and 2-colorable 3-uniform hypergraph H, we have
                                                                      
                                  val Game2b[H, k, ℓ2 , t, q0 , q1 , β] = 1.

                                                                        47
Proof. We fix strategies from Lemma 5.10 that achieve value 1. Alice ignores the lists Aj and
answers each block j ∈ [t] separately according to her strategy from Game2a. Bob answers according
to Alice’s strategy for Game2a on the noncritical blocks j ∈ [t]\{c}, and according to Bob’s strategy
for Game2a on the critical block j = c.

    The following lemma establishes the soundness of Game2b.

Lemma 5.22 (Game2b soundness). Assume Condition 3.12. Suppose that in every 4r -coloring of
H, at least ε fraction of the edges are monochromatic. Then we have
                                                                  
                              val Game2b[H, k, ℓ2 , t, q0 , q1 , β] ⩽ 2−q2 /4 .

Proof. It suffices to show that
                                                                                       
                  val Game2b[H, k, ℓ2 , t, q0 , q1 , β] ⩽ val Game2a[H, k, ℓ2 , q1 , 0, β] ,

after which the conclusion follows from Lemma 5.19 (combined with Remark 5.3).
    Assume Alice and Bob have a strategy for Game2b[H, k, ℓ2 , t, q0 , q1 , β], and we next show how
to derive a strategy for Game2a[H, k, ℓ2 , q1 , 0, β]. In the latter game, Alice and Bob can first agree
upon the following “shared data” using public randomness:

    • a uniformly random critical index c ∈ [t], and

    • a uniformly random tuple (Ej , Aj , Uj ) for each non-critical index j ∈ [t] \ {c}. More precisely,
                                                                   E       E q
      sample Ej from E (H, k) uniformly, sample Aj from F2 j ⊗ F2 j 0 uniformly, and sample Uj
      by drawing (Ejghost , empty, empty, Uj ) ∼ Jmid (Ej , 0, 0, β, ℓ2 ).

Given a question (E, A, U ) in Game2a[H, k, ℓ2 , q1 , 0, β], Alice can construct (E, A, U) by planting
(E, A, U ) in the critical block c and using the shared data for the non-critical blocks. Similarly, given
a question (E ′ , A′ , U ′ ), Bob can construct (E ′ , A′ , U ′ ) by planting (E ′ , A′ , U ′ ) in the critical block c
and using the shared data for the non-critical blocks. The provers can then pretend that they are
in Game2b[H, k, ℓ2 , t, q0 , q1 , β], and use their strategy for Game2b[H, k, ℓ2 , t, q0 , q1 , β] to answer the
verifier in Game2a[H, k, ℓ2 , q1 , 0, β]. Whenever the strategy for Game2b[H, k, ℓ2 , t, q0 , q1 , β] provides
consistent answers to (E, A, U) and (c, E ′ , A′ , U ′ ), the consistency in the critical block implies that
the derived answers to the questions (E, A, U ) and (E ′ , A′ , U ′ ) in Game2a[H, k, ℓ2 , q1 , 0, β] are also
consistent. Therefore, the verifier in Game2a[H, k, ℓ2 , q1 , 0, β] accepts with probability at least the
acceptance probability in Game2b[H, k, ℓ2 , t, q0 , q1 , β].


6     The Inner PCP
In this section we construct and analyze our inner PCP as discussed in Section 2.2.6. We first
prepare some necessary framework in Sections 6.1 and 6.2. The final sub-4-to-1 game will then be
defined in Section 6.3.

6.1    Preparation: Tripled Packets
In the inner PCP, we will add another layer of Grassmann-type structure onto Game2b, which is
already somewhat complicated. To make future notations more concise, in this subsection we set
up some notational framework for the objects featured in Game2b (Definition 5.20).


                                                          48
    Recall that in Definition 5.20, for each index j ∈ [t], Alice needs to return a bilinear map
     E      E
f : F2 j ⊗ F2 j → F2 that is both symmetric and NAE-satisfying. The following definition combines
the two conditions, exploiting the fact that both are “linear” conditions in f .

Definition 6.1. Let E be a tripled set. For any v ∈ E, we let ev ∈ FE     2 denote the standard basis
vector supported on v; that is, for u ∈ E let (ev )u = 1 if and only if u = v.

 (1) We define N1 (E) ⊆ FE    E
                         2 ⊗ F2 to be the linear subspace spanned by all vectors of the form

                          ev1 ⊗ ev1 + ev2 ⊗ ev2 + ev3 ⊗ ev3 + ev1 ⊗ ev2 + ev2 ⊗ ev3 + ev3 ⊗ ev1 ,

         where {v1 , v2 , v3 } ranges in Triples(E).11

 (2) We let N2 (E) ⊆ FE    E
                      2 ⊗ F2 be the linear subspace spanned by all anti-symmetric vectors, i.e.

                            N2 (E) = span x ⊗ y + y ⊗ x x, y ∈ FE
                                           
                                                                  2 .


 (3) Let N (E) = N1 (E) + N2 (E) be the sum of the two subspaces.

 (4) A linear map f : N (E) → F2 is said to be SymNAE-satisfying if we have:

             • f (ev1 ⊗ ev1 + ev2 ⊗ ev2 + ev3 ⊗ ev3 + ev1 ⊗ ev2 + ev2 ⊗ ev3 + ev3 ⊗ ev1 ) = 1 for all triples
               {v1 , v2 , v3 } ∈ Triples(E); and
             • f (x ⊗ y + y ⊗ x) = 0 for all x, y ∈ FE
                                                     2.

    Intuitively, the space N1 (E) from the first item above is the subspace of FE    E
                                                                                2 ⊗ F2 on which the
                               E     E
value of a bilinear form f : F2 ⊗ F2 → F2 is fully determined if it is NAE-satisfying. The space
N2 (E) from the second item above is the subspace of FE           E
                                                           2 ⊗ F2 on which the value of a bilinear
            E     E
form f : F2 ⊗ F2 → F2 is fully determined if it is symmetric. Thus, overall, whether f is both
NAE-satisfying and symmetric is fully captured by its value on N (E), and this is the definition of
SymNAE-satisfying.
    The following proposition asserts that there is always a linear map on N (E) which is SymNAE-
satisfying.

Proposition 6.2. For any tripled set E, there is a unique linear map f : N (E) → F2 that is
SymNAE-satisfying.

Proof. This is clear because N1 (E) ∩ N2 (E) = {0}.

Definition 6.7. Given a tripled packet (E, U) where E = (E1 , . . . , Et ) and U = (U1 , . . . , Ut ), a
linear map f : N (E, U) → F2 is said to be SNR-satisfying if we have:
   • f |N (Ej ) is SymNAE-satisfying for each j ∈ [t].

   • rank(f |Uj ⊗Uj ) ⩽ 1 for each j ∈ [t].

6.1.1   Thinning of Tripled Packets
Recall that in Game2b, we plant the true challenge in one randomly chosen coordinate, and it plays
a crucial role in our argument. We often refer to it as the critical index/coordinate/block, and make
use of the following notations.
Definition 6.8. Let (E1 , . . . , Et ) be a sequence of tripled sets. Fix an index c ∈ [t] and a parameter
β ∈ [0, 1]. We write E ′ ∼ Thinc,β (E) for the random sequence E ′ = (E1′ , . . . , Et′ ) obtained by setting
Ej′ = Ej for all j ∈ [t] \ {c} and sampling Ec′ ∼ Thinβ (Ec ).
    The following definition captures the distribution of (U, E ′ , U ′ ) conditioned on E in Defini-
tion 5.20. Note that to describe our final PCP, we will not need the various advice features we
incorporated (they are crucial however for the soundness analysis).
Definition 6.9. Let E = (E1 , . . . , Et ) be a sequence of tripled sets. Fix a parameter β ∈ [0, 1] and
a nonnegative integer ℓ. We let Jin (E, β, ℓ) denote the output distribution of the following process:

                                                     50
   1. Sample a uniformly random index c ∈ [t]. Sample (Ec′ , empty, empty, Uc ) ∼ Jmid (Ec , 0, 0, β, ℓ).
                                   E′
      Sample a random vector x ∈ F2 c and let Uc′ = Uc + span(x).

   2. For each j ∈ [t] \ {c}, sample (Ejghost , empty, empty, Uj ) ∼ Jmid (Ej , 0, 0, β, ℓ). Let Ej′ = Ej
      and Uj′ = Uj .

   3. Let E ′ = (E1′ , . . . , Et′ ), U = (U1 , . . . , Ut ) and U ′ = (U1′ , . . . , Ut′ ).

   4. Output the tuple (c, U, E ′ , U ′ ).

    We will make use of the following simple observation:

Proposition 6.10. In the setting and notation of Definition 6.9, the random subspaces U1 , . . . , Ut
are mutually independent. Furthermore, they are jointly independent of the random index c.

Proof. Regardless of whether j = c or j ̸= c, the subspace Uj is spanned by ℓ independent vectors
supported on a random β-thinning of Ej .

6.2     Preparation: Low-Rank Grassmann
As discussed in Section 2.2.6, in the inner PCP we want to use linear subspaces of FE        Ec
                                                                                       2 ⊗ F2 that
                                                                                        c

consist only of low-rank vectors. As stated in Definition 2.6, these linear subspaces will form a
subset LGr(FE               Ec    Ec
              2 , ℓ) of Gr(F2 ⊗ F2 , ℓ), and the subgraph it induces from the Grassmann graph is
               c

called the low-rank Grassmann graph. In this subsection, we give formal definitions of the low-rank
Grassmann and prove a number of elementary facts about it. We will primarily be interested in
the basic “statistical” properties of LGr(FE c
                                            2 , ℓ), such as its zoom-out probabilities being close to
those of the full Grassmann graph:

                                           P       [L ⊆ W ] ≈            P       [L ⊆ W ] ,            (6.1)
                                      L∈LGr(V,ℓ)                  L∈Gr(V ⊗V,ℓ)

for any vector space V and subspace W ⊆ V ⊗ V with small codimension. More sophisticated
analysis of the low-rank Grassmann graph, such as its spectral expansion (see [Gol23]), will not be
used in this paper.

6.2.1     Construction from Hadamard Code
The definition of the low-rank Grassmann will use linear combinations of vectors given by the
Hadamard matrix, defined as follows.

Definition 6.11. Let ℓ be a positive integer. For each i ∈ {0, 1, . . . , 2ℓ −1} and b ∈ {0, 1, . . . , ℓ−1},
define Bitℓ (i, b) ∈ F2 to be the mod-2 remainder of the integer ⌊i · 2−b ⌋. Equivalently, the sequence
                                                                                     
                                Bitℓ (i, ℓ − 1), Bitℓ (i, ℓ − 2), . . . , Bitℓ (i, 0)

is the length-ℓ binary representation of i, with the most significant bit listed first.
    For each integer i ∈ {0, 1, . . . , 2ℓ − 1} and vector u ∈ Fℓ2 , define

be convenient for us for technical reasons).

Remark 6.13. In the setting and notation of Definition 6.12, suppose that the odd-indexed vectors
a(1) , a(3) , . . . , a(q−1) are linearly independent, and that the even-indexed vectors a(2) , a(4) , . . . , a(q)
are also linearly independent. Then for any u ∈ Fℓ2 \ {0}, since Hadℓ (u, i) = 1 for exactly 2ℓ−1 of
the i’s in {1, 2, . . . , 2ℓ − 1}, it follows from Equation (6.2) that

                                         rank (Lvec(A, ℓ, u)) = 2ℓ−1 s.

Furthermore, it is easy to see that dim(Lspan(A, ℓ, Fℓ2 )) = ℓ.

6.2.2    Mixed-Type Advice Sequences
Our inner PCP will include a tensor product of the low-rank Grassmann graph (on the critical
block) with the standard Grassmann graph (on non-critical blocks). In this section, we present a
few notations that will be useful in presenting and working with the resulting product space.

                                                          52
Definition 6.14. Given a sequence of finite sets E ′ = (E1′ , . . . , Et′ ), a subset of indices J ⊆ [t] and
positive integers ℓ, s, we let AJ (E ′ , ℓ, s) be the collection of advice sequences (A1 , . . . , At ) such that:

 (1) For each j ∈ [t] \ J, the component Aj is a sequence of ℓ vectors Aj = (a(j,1) , . . . , a(j,ℓ) ), where
                                                   E′      E′
                                         a(j,i) ∈ F2 j ⊗ F2 j        for all i ∈ [ℓ].

 (2) For each c ∈ J, the component Ac is a sequence of q = 2s(2ℓ −1) vectors Ac = (a(c,1) , . . . , a(c,q) ),
     where
                                                       E′
                                           a(c,i) ∈ F2 c     for all i ∈ [q].
     If both sequences a(c,1) , a(c,3) , . . . , a(c,q−1) and a(c,2) , a(c,4) , . . . , a(c,q) are linearly indepen-
                                                                                             

     dent, we say that Ac is saturated.

We let AJ∗ (E ′ , ℓ, s) denote the sub-collection of advice sequences (A1 , . . . , At ) ∈ AJ (E ′ , ℓ, s) such
that Ac is saturated for each c ∈ J.

Remark 6.15. A few remarks are in order:

 (1) The set of coordinates in J in Definition 6.14 should be thought of as the potentially critical
                                                                                                             E′
      coordinates, hence the collection there is specified by vectors from the base space F2 j . The
      coordinates outside J are not critical, and the vectors there are completely arbitrary vectors
              E′      E′
      from F2 j ⊗ F2 j .

 (2) An advice sequence in AJ (E ′ , ℓ, s) should be thought of as the analog of a spanning set of
     a subspace. In other words, there may be linear dependencies between vectors in a member
     from AJ (E ′ , ℓ, s). In our setting of parameters this will be rare (analogously to the fact that
     a random set of o(n) independently chosen vectors in Fn2 are linearly independent with high
     probability), and AJ∗ (E ′ , ℓ, s) ⊆ AJ (E ′ , ℓ, s) is the subcollection that corresponds to tuples
     with no linear dependencies.

   The following definition associates vectors from V(E ′ ) with each advice sequence. In words, on
each potentially critical coordinate we take the low-rank Hadamard span as per Definition 6.12,
and on non-critical coordinates we take the span for arbitrary vectors as per Equation (6.3).

subspaces. Towards this end, it is useful to note that if we considered Equation (6.3) and only
looked at vectors u with uℓ = 0, then we would get an (ℓ − 1)-dimensional space which is a subspace
of the larger ℓ-dimensional space resulting from taking all u. Moreover, this subspace would not
                       ℓ
depend on what is x(2 −2) (as its coefficient will always be 0). Generalizing this intuition, we get
the following analogous notion of sub-advice.
Definition 6.18. Fix a sequence of finite sets E ′ = (E1′ , . . . , Et′ ), a proper subset of indices J ⊊ [t],
positive integers ℓ, s and a nonnegative integer q0 ⩽ ℓ. Given an advice sequence A = (A1 , . . . , At ) ∈
AJ (E ′ , ℓ, s), we define a sub-advice A[q0 ] = (A′1 , . . . , A′t ) ∈ AJ (E ′ , q0 , 2ℓ−q0 s) as follows:

 (1) For each j ∈ [t] \ J, write Aj = a(j,1) , . . . , a(j,ℓ) and let A′j = a(j,1) , . . . , a(j,q0 ) .
                                                                                                         

                                                          

       and let A′c = a(c,1) , . . . , a(c,q1 ) .
                                              

Notation 6.20. Throughout the remainder of the paper, the set J ⊆ [t] of critical indices will
always satisfy |J| ∈ {1, 2}. When J = {c1 , c2 } with c1 ̸= c2 , we suppress the braces and write c1 , c2
in place of {c1 , c2 } whenever J appears as a subscript. For example, we write

Similarly, when J = {c}, we write, for instance,

6.2.3    Zoom-out Probabilities
We next prove a few elementary properties of the “zoom-out probabilities” associated with mixed-
type advice sequences. Specifically, given a linear subspace W ⊆ V(E ′ ) of small codimension, we
aim to show that the probability that σ(A) ⊆ W , where A is sampled at random from AJ (E ′ , ℓ, s),
is close to the probability that a random subspace L ∈ Gr(V(E ′ ), ℓ) is contained in W .
    For technical reasons, we also need a “zoomed-in” version of this comparison. Specifically, we
consider a random advice sequence A ∈ AJ (E ′ , ℓ, s) conditioned on σ(A[q0 ] ) = Q, where Q ⊆ V(E ′ )
is a fixed q0 -dimensional subspace. This should be analogous to sampling a random subspace
L ∈ Gr(V(E ′ ), ℓ) conditioned on Q ⊆ L. Our main goal is to argue that
                                                    
                     P        σ(A) ⊆ W σ(A[q0 ] ) = Q ≈       P      [L ⊆ W | Q ⊆ L] .

    Given A′ ∈ AJ (E ′ , ℓ − 1, 2s) such that σ(A′ ) ⊆ W , Proposition 6.22 estimates the probability
of that random extension A ∈ AJ (E ′ , ℓ, s) of A′ still “stays in” W , in the sense that σ(A) ⊆ W .
Roughly speaking, this means that “random extension” by 1 dimension from σ(A[ℓ−1] ) to σ(A)
behaves nicely with respect to zoom-outs. This allows us to iterate the process and show that a
random extension by ℓ − q0 dimensions from σ(A[q0 ] ) to σ(A) also behaves as expected with respect
to zoom-outs.

                                                               57
6.2.4        Mixed-Type Grassmann Graph
So far, we have mainly worked with uniformly random mixed-type advice sequences A ∈ AJ (E ′ , ℓ, s),
the vectors in which may have linear dependencies (though unlikely). It will be useful later in the
analysis of our inner PCP to consider those A with no linear dependency among their vectors,
spanning actual ℓ-dimensional subspaces σ(A) ⊆ V(E ′ ).
    This naturally leads us to the sub-collection A∗J (E ′ , ℓ, s) defined in Definition 6.14. For sequences
A = (A1 , . . . , At ) ∈ A∗J (E ′ , ℓ, s), the lists Ac in the critical blocks c ∈ J are always saturated,
and by Remark 6.13, it follows that dim σ(A) = ℓ (as long as J is nonempty). Therefore, these
subspaces σ(A) lie in the domain Gr(V(E ′ ), ℓ), and they induce a subgraph of the Grassmann graph
Gr(V(E ′ ), ℓ). We call this subgraph a “mixed-type Grassmann graph,” as defined below.

Definition 6.24. Fix a sequence of finite sets E ′ = (E1′ , . . . , Et′ ), a nonempty subset of indices
J ⊊ [t] and positive integers ℓ, s. Define MGrJ (E ′ , ℓ, s) to be the following collection of linear
subspaces:
                    MGrJ (E ′ , ℓ, s) = σ(A) A ∈ AJ∗ (E ′ , ℓ, s) ⊆ Gr(V(E ′ ), ℓ).
                                       

    An important fact (whose proof we omit) is that for different subspaces L ∈ MGrJ (E ′ , ℓ, s),
the number of A ∈ AJ∗ (E ′ , ℓ, s) such that σ(A) = L is the same.14 Therefore, if we sample a
uniformly random A ∈ AJ∗ (E ′ , ℓ, s), the span σ(A) is uniformly random over MGrJ (E ′ , ℓ, s). The
next proposition asserts that even if one chose A from AJ (E ′ , ℓ, s), the resulting distribution of
σ(A) is still close to uniform over MGrJ (E ′ , ℓ, s), since |A∗J (E ′ , ℓ, s)| = (1 − o(1)) · |AJ (E ′ , ℓ, s)|.

Proposition 6.25. Fix a sequence of non-empty finite sets E ′ = (E1′ , . . . , Et′ ), a nonempty subset
of indices J ⊊ [t], positive integers ℓ, s and a nonnegative integer q0 < ℓ. Assume that |Ec′ | ⩾ 2ℓ+2 s
for each c ∈ J. For any fixed advice sequence A′ ∈ AJ∗ (E ′ , q0 , 2ℓ−q0 s), if we sample A ∈ AJ (E ′ , ℓ, s)
conditioned on σ(A[q0 ] ) = σ(A′ ), the resulting distribution of σ(A) has total variation distance at
most

6.2.5    Extension of Agreement
The next somewhat technical result is needed in Section 6.5.4, and to explain it we first give the
analog in the simpler Grassmann setting. Suppose we have a table F that assigns to each subspace
in Gr(Fn2 , ℓ + 1) a linear function F [L] (or nil), a subspace W ⊆ Fn2 of constant codimension, and
a linear function f : W → F2 that largely agrees with F in the sense that
                                              h                  i
                                       Pn      f |L∩W = F [L]|L∩W ⩾ δ.
                                     L∈Gr(F2 ,ℓ+1)

We wish to extend f into a linear function g : Fn2 → F2 that agrees with F not only on the
intersection with W . Then a random extension would suffice: for any L such that f |L∩W =
F [L]|L∩W we will have that g|L = F [L] with constant probability. The following lemma establishes
an analogous statement in the context of mixed-type subspaces.
Lemma 6.27. Fix a sequence of non-empty finite sets E ′ = (E1′ , . . . , Et′ ), a critical index c ∈ [t],
positive integers ℓ, s, nonnegative integers q0 , w and a parameter δ ∈ (0, 1). Assume

each linear subspace L ⊆ V(E ′ ), and there exists a linear function f : W → F2 such that

6.3      The 4-to-1 Game Construction
In this subsection, we use the framework prepared in Sections 6.1 and 6.2 to construct our final
sub-4-to-1 game. Before defining the sub-4-to-1 game, we first rewrite the description of Game2b
(defined in Section 5.4) using the notations introduced in Sections 6.1 and 6.2.

Definition 6.28. Let H be a 3-uniform hypergraph. Given positive integers k and t, we let
E (H, k, t) denote the collection of all sequences E = (E1 , . . . , Et ) such that Ej ∈ E (H, k) for all
j ∈ [t].

      We can now rewrite the definition of Game2b[H, k, ℓ2 , t, q0 , q1 , β] as follows.

Definition 6.29. Given positive integers k, ℓ2 , t, s1 , a nonnegative integer q0 , a parameter β ∈ [0, 1)
and a 3-uniform hypergraph H, let Game2c[H, k, ℓ2 , t, s1 , q0 , β] be a 2-prover 1-round game where
the verifier executes the following:

   1. Sample a uniformly random sequence E = (E1 , . . . , Et ) ∈ E (H, k, t).

   2. Sample (c, U, E ′ , U ′ ) ∼ Jin (E, β, ℓ2 ).

   3. Sample a uniformly random sequence A = (A1 , . . . , At ) ∈ Ac (E ′ , q0 , s1 ).15

   4. Send (E, A, U) to Alice and (c, E ′ , A, U ′ ) to Bob.

   5. Alice must return a linear map f : V(E) → F2 such that f |N (E,U ) is SNR-satisfying; Bob must
      return a linear map g : V(E ′ ) → F2 . Accept if f |V(E ′ ) = g.

    It is easy to observe that Definition 6.29 defines the same game as substituting q1 = 2s1 (2q0 − 1)
in Definition 5.20 does.

Proposition 6.30. For any positive integers k, ℓ2 , t, s1 , nonnegative integer q0 , parameter β ∈ [0, 1)
and 3-uniform hypergraph H, we have
                                                                                              
          val Game2b[H, k, ℓ2 , t, q0 , 2s1 (2q0 − 1), β] = val Game2c[H, k, ℓ2 , t, s1 , q0 , β] .
 15
      If q0 = 0, we take A = empty to be an empty sequence.



                                                                 61
Proof. It is easy to see the equivalence between Game2b and Game2c by comparing Definitions 5.20
and 6.29.

    We are now ready to define our sub-4-to-1 game based on Game2c. The main idea here is that
instead of providing the functions f, g fully as in the game in Game2c, the provers are expected to
provide an encoding of them based on the mixed-type Grassmann graph. More specifically, each
player receives a linear subspace of their ambient space, and is requested to respond by a linear
function on it. In order to make the game sub-4-to-1, the subspace Alice receives is contained in
the subspace Bob receives and is of 1 less dimension, and additionally we incorporate inside it the
local tensor tests as well as the side-condition equations.

Definition 6.31. Given positive integers k, ℓ1 , ℓ2 , t, s, a parameter β ∈ [0, 1) and a 3-uniform
hypergraph H, let Game3[H, k, ℓ1 , ℓ2 , t, s, β] be a 2-prover 1-round game where the verifier executes
the following:

   1. Sample a uniformly random sequence E = (E1 , . . . , Et ) ∈ E (H, k, t).

   2. Sample (c, U, E ′ , U ′ ) ∼ Jin (E, β, ℓ2 ).

   3. Sample a uniformly random sequence A = (A1 , . . . , At ) ∈ Ac (E ′ , 2ℓ1 , s). Let

                            L = σ(A[ℓ1 ] ) ⊆ V(E ′ )   and     L′ = σ(A[ℓ1 +1] ) ⊆ V(E ′ ).

   4. Send (E, U, L + N (E, U)) to Alice and (c, E ′ , U ′ , L′ +       U ′ ) to Bob.
                                                                    L

   5. Alice must return a linear map f : (L + N (E, U)) → F2 such that f |N (E,U ) is SNR-satisfying;
      Bob must return a linear map g : (L′ + U ′ ) → F2 such that g|Ui′ ⊗Ui′ is symmetric and rank
                                              L
      at most 1 for each i. Accept if g|(L+L U ) = f |(L+L U ) .

   The game defined in Definition 6.31 is clearly an “at most 4-to-1” game, in the sense that for
any response of Alice there are at most 4 responses for Bob which would make the verifier accept.
Indeed, this is because L ⊆ L′ is of one less dimension, and U, U ′ are identical on all coordinates,
except for the critical coordinate i ∈ [t] where Ui ⊆ Ui′ has one less dimension. Thus, Bob has at
most 2 more bits of freedom than Alice. It is not difficult to turn this “at most 4-to-1” game to an
actual 4-to-1 game as per Definition 1.1; see Appendix B.
   The completeness of Game3 is straightforward and is given in the following lemma.

Lemma 6.32 (Game3 completeness). For any positive integers k, ℓ1 , ℓ2 , t, s, a parameter β ∈ [0, 1)
and a 2-colorable 3-uniform hypergraph H, we have
                                                                
                               val Game3[H, k, ℓ1 , ℓ2 , t, s, β] = 1.

Proof. By Lemma 5.21 and Proposition 6.30, there is a set of strategies for Alice and Bob that
achieve value 1 in Game2c[H, k, ℓ2 , t, s1 , 0, β] (here we may take s1 = 1, but it is irrelevant anyway).
Note that since the second-to-last parameter in Game2c[H, k, ℓ2 , t, s1 , 0, β] is set to be zero, there
is no advice in this game. Suppose the strategies are represented by a table F [·] for Alice and a
table G[·] for Bob, where F [E, U] : V(E) → F2 is a linear map for each Alice-question (E, U), and
G[c, E ′ , U ′ ] : V(E ′ ) → F2 is a linear map for each Bob-question (c, E ′ , U ′ ).
    We can then construct a strategy for Alice in the game Game3[H, k, ℓ1 , ℓ2 , t, s, β] as follows:

   1. Receive the question (E, U, L + N (E, U)).

                                                       62
   2. Let f be the restriction of F [E, U] to L + N (E, U), and return f . Note that by Definition 6.29,
      the restriction of F [E, U] to N (E, U) must be SNR-satisfying.
Similarly, the strategy for Bob is:
   1. Receive the question (c, E ′ , U ′ , L′ +       U ′ ).
                                                  L

   2. Let g be the restriction of G[c, E ′ , U ′ ] to L′ +              U ′.
                                                                    L

Since we always have F [E, U]|V(E ′ ) = G[c, E ′ , U ′ ], it is clear that we also have f |(L+L U ) = g|(L+L U ) .
Moreover, we choose F, G to be the strategies induced from the proper 2-coloring of the original
graph through the series of reductions. The strategies are symmetric and have rank at most 1 re-
stricted to each block. Therefore, under this set of strategies, the verifier of Game3[H, k, ℓ1 , ℓ2 , t, s, β]
accepts with probability 1.

    The soundness of Game3 is stated in the following lemma.
Lemma 6.33 (Game3 soundness). Assume Condition 3.12. Suppose that in every 4r -coloring of
H, at least ε fraction of the edges are monochromatic. Then we have
                                                                  
                                 val Game3[H, k, ℓ1 , ℓ2 , t, s, β] ⩽ δ.

    The proof of Lemma 6.33 is significantly more involved, and spans the rest of this section. To-
wards this end, we first establish preliminary results in Section 6.5, and then use them in Section 6.6
to complete the proof of Lemma 6.33.

6.4    Overview of Soundness Analysis
In this section we discuss the overview of the analysis of the construction from Definition 6.31.
Let F , G be strategies of Alice and Bob respectively that achieve value at least δ. Our goal is to
show how to derive from F and G strategies for Game2c from Definition 6.29 achieving a significant
value. At a high-level, the proof proceeds via the following steps:
   1. A decoding strategy for Alice: recall that for each (E, U, L + N (E, U)), Alice’s strategy
      provides a linear function F [E, U, L + N (E, U)] : L + N (E, U) → F2 satisfying the side condi-
      tions on N (E, U). Thus, for each fixing of E, U, we can uniquely define FeE,U that maps a linear
      subspace L such that L ∩ N (E, U) = {0}, to F [E, U, L + N (E, U)]|L . Using the consistency
      between F and G and the Cauchy-Schwarz inequality in a similar way to Lemma 5.15, it
      can be shown that on average, FeE,U passes the Grassmann agreement test with noticeable
      probability. At this point, we would like to apply Theorem 3.5 to be able to decode a global
      linear function for Alice (inside some zoom-in/zoom-out pair), but the distribution over L
      induced by the construction is not exactly uniform over V(E) (due to the critical coordinate).
      However, it turns out that this distribution is close to being uniform over subspaces in V(E);
      we refer to this as covering property for the mixed-type subspaces.
      Ultimately, we conclude that there is a set of constant dimension zoom ins Q in V(E) of
      noticeable fractional size, such that for each Q ∈ Q there is a zoom-out W ⊆ V(E) of constant
      codimension, such that FeE,U agrees with some global linear function fE,U ,Q,W : W → F2 on
      a noticeable fraction of Q ⊆ L ⊆ W . Translating this information back to F [E, U, ·] gives a
      global linear function that also satisfies the side conditions and the tensor tests.
      Alice in Game2c can utilize the above observation to construct a strategy. More precisely,
      she chooses Q using her Advice, and chooses W for which a global linear function as above

                                                               63
        exists. Strictly speaking, she has to choose W as maximal subspace with respect to inclusion
        such that a global linear function as above exists; this is done so as to ensure that for a fixed
        Q, we have that the list of possible answers Alice gives is small.

  2. A decoding strategy for Bob: by appropriate pruning, it is easy to ensure that a decoding
     strategy for Alice automatically gives a decoding strategy for Bob. Indeed, if we only keep
     entries (E, U, L + N (E, U)) of Alice such that conditioned on them, Alice and Bob win with
     probability at least δ/2, then any decoding of Alice automatically has quite good agreement
     with the strategy of Bob but in a little bit of a strange way. Indeed, because Alice’s space
     has 1 less dimension than Bob’s, we conclude that Bob’s table G and Alice’s global function
     f satisfy the premise of Lemma 6.27, and using it we are able to get a decoding for Bob.

  3. Consistency of the decodings: let us assume now that we have fixed a zoom-in Q, so that
     Alice has a list of possible zoom-out global function pairs (f1 , W1 ), . . . , (fm , Wm ), and Bob has
     the list (g1 , W1′ ), . . . , (gN , WN′ ). A natural idea to prove the consistency between decodings
     is to show that the list of legitimate decodings of Alice and Bob are of bounded size. Such
     a result for Alice’s table was proved in [DKK+ 25], but it is much more complicated for Bob
     since the structure of mixed-type Grassmann is quite different from the ordinary Grassmann.
     While we believe this is true, we do not address this problem directly, and instead bypass this
     difficulty using the following idea.
        Using the same argument as in step 2 (but in the reverse direction), we know that for a large
        number of Alice-packets (E, U), (gi , Wi′ ) also has good consistency with Alice’s table, but
        also in the similar mixed-type Grassmann domain instead of our desired ordinary Grassmann
        domain as described in Alice’s strategy. This is formalized by Definition 6.59 and Claim 6.61.
        The heart of the argument is to show that, because there are many such Bob-packets, there
        must be some global consistency between them. More precisely, we show that there is some
        i such that gi (or its linear extension) has consistency with Alice’s table in the ordinary
        Grassmann domain rather than mixed-type Grassmann, and we elaborate on this step in Sec-
        tion 6.8.
        Using this observation, while it does not say that Bob’s list is bounded, it does say that we can
        conclude that for every Alice-packet (E, U) and advice Q, if its table has noticeable consistency
        with respect to mixed-type Grassmann for a lot of Bob-packets (E ′ , U ′ ) and the corresponding
        decodings (f, W ), then, many of them would also have consistency with F [E, U, ·] in the
        ordinary Grassmann domain, so the responses of Alice and Bob are consistent with noticeable
        probability.

6.5     Preparatory Lemmas for Soundness Analysis
In this subsection, we fix a 3-uniform hypergraph H, positive integers k, ℓ1 , ℓ2 , t, s, a nonnegative
integer q0 and a parameter β ∈ (0, 1). We consider the game Game3[H, k, ℓ1 , ℓ2 , t, s, β] as defined
in Definition 6.31.

6.5.2    Covering Property of Mixed-Type Subspaces
Note that in Definition 6.31, the subspace L that is being sent to Alice is not uniformly chosen from
her space V(E), and instead it is chosen from V(E ′ ) with a random critical coordinate c. For the test
we perform to make sense from Alice’s perspective (as well as for the advice feature we arranged to
work) it is crucial that the distribution of L is statistically close to being uniform over subspaces
of her ambient space. The following definition and lemma make this assertion more precise, and in
fact they show that the two distributions are close even conditioning on a small number of fixed
vectors being contained in L.

                                                          67
Definition 6.45. For a fixed Alice-packet (E, U), a linear subspace Q ⊆ V(E) is said to be smooth

Corollary 6.46. Assume Condition 3.12. For any fixed Alice-packet (E, U), we have
                         h                                         i

Proof. Immediate from Lemma 6.44 and Markov’s inequality.
    Finally, we record a covering property without advice, meaning that the distribution of L in

(E, U, L, ∗, ∗, ∗, ∗) ∼ Din (without conditioning on Q) is close to uniform over Gr(V(E), ℓ1 ).

Proof. The proof is entirely analogous to the (combined) proofs of Lemmas 6.40 and 6.43, and is
hence omitted. The main difference is that when one invokes Proposition F.1, this time one can
take Bj to be a singleton set for each j ∈ [t].

    In conclusion, we have shown that if the F, G are Alice and Bob tables that agree with noticeable
probability, then with noticeable probability Alice can use her zoom-in Q to come up with a global
linear function f agreeing. We next show a similar assertion to Bob.

6.5.4     Bob’s Existence of Decoding
We intend to show that Bob is able to come up with a response using the fact that Alice can, and
that Alice’s space “covers” Bob’s space. Towards this end, we make the following definitions. The
first definition gives a precise formulation of the packets of Bob that “inherit” good agreement from
a global linear function of Alice.
Definition 6.51. Let G[·] be a Bob table, and let (E ′ , U ′ ) be a Bob-packet with critical index
c ∈ [t]. For any linear subspace Q ⊆ V(E ′ ) with dim Q ⩽ q0 , we say that the tuple (E ′ , U ′ , Q) is
pre-answerable with respect to G[·] if the following statements hold:
                                                                          +
  1. The tuple (∗, ∗, ∗, Q, E ′ , U ′ , ∗) has nonzero probability under Din . In other words, Q = σ(A)
     for some A ∈ Ac (E , q0 , 2

                         h                                                          i

             A∈Ac (E ′ ,ℓ1 +1,s′ )

        where s′ = 2ℓ1 −1 s.
    The next definition formalizes the type of pruning we need for the strategies of Alice and Bob
to be able to convert a global function of Alice to Bob and vice versa.
Definition 6.52. Let η ∈ (0, 1) be a threshold parameter. An Alice-table F [·] and a Bob-table
G[·] are said to be η-transferable (with each other) if the following statements hold:

                                                        Vrf F,G (∗, ∗, ∗, E ′ , U ′ , L′ ) ⩾ η.

                                                                   72
   The following proposition shows that a pre-answerable packet is typically also an answerable
packet.
Proposition 6.55. Assume Condition 3.12. Let F [·] be an Alice-table, and let G[·] be a Bob-
table that is 2−q0 -transferable with F [·]. Suppose (E ′ , U ′ ) is a Bob-packet with critical index c and
Q ∈ MGrc (E ′ , q0 , 22ℓ1 −q0 s) is a subspace such that
 (1) |Ec′ | ⩾ 3k/2, and
 (2) (E ′ , U ′ , Q) is pre-answerable with respect to G[·].
Then (E ′ , U ′ , Q) is answerable with respect to G[·].
Proof. By Proposition 6.25, the total variation distance between the following two distributions of
L′ is at most, say, k −2 :
 (1) sample L′ ∈ MGrc (E ′ , ℓ1 + 1, 2ℓ1 −1 s) conditioned on Q ⊆ L′ ; and
 (2) sample A ∈ Ac (E ′ , ℓ1 + 1, 2ℓ1 −1 s) conditioned on σ(A[q0 ] ) = Q, and let L′ = σ(A).
We know from Proposition 6.26 that even if we further condition both distributions on {L′ ⊆ W },
for any linear subspace W ⊆ V(E ′ ) of codimension at most q0 , the resulting distributions are still
k −1 -close in total variation distance. By definition, this allows one to convert pre-answerability to
answerability.

6.6    Soundness Analysis
Now, we have all ingredients to analyze the soundness of Game3.

Proof of Lemma 6.33. Assume that
                                                             
                            val Game3[H, k, ℓ1 , ℓ2 , t, s, β] > δ,

the goal is to show that
                                                                     
                                 val Game2c[H, k, ℓ2 , t, s1 , q0 , β] > 2−q2 /4 ,

which leads to a contradiction to Lemma 5.22.
      Recall that the answers returned by Alice and Bob in Game3 are “folded”. We define the follow-
ing folded tables Fe, G  e for Alice and Bob. Suppose that the verifier samples (E, U, L, ∗, E ′ , U ′ , L′ ) ∼
   +
Din   . Then the question sent to Alice is (E, U, L + N (E, U)) and she returns a linear function
f on L + N (E,    L U).    Then, we define Fe[E, U, L + N (E, U)] := f . Similarly, G  e is defined by
       ′     ′  ′      ′
G[E , U , L +L U ] := g if g is the linear function returned by Bob upon receiving the question
 e
(E ′ , U ′ , L′ + U ′ ).

Getting unfolded tables. To make use of the decoding lemmas (Lemmas 6.50 and 6.53), we
need to define “unfolded” tables F ∗ [·], G∗ [·]

                                    F ∗ [E, U, L] := Fe[E, U, L + N (E, U)]|L ,

and
                                                            h                M i
                                  G∗ [E ′ , U ′ , L′ ] := G
                                                          e E ′ , U ′ , L′ +  U ′ |L′ ,

                                                           +
for every possible sample (E, U, L, ∗, E ′ , U ′ , L′ ) ∼ Din . For other unfolded questions (E, U, L), (E ′ , U ′ , L′ ),
we simply define F ∗ [E, U, L] := nil and G∗ [E ′ , U ′ , L′ ] := nil.

Getting transferable tables via pruning. To use Lemma 6.53, we also want our table to be
transferable. We use the following process to get transferable tables F [·], G[·] from F ∗ , G∗ .
Definition 6.57 (Pruned tables). We use the following process to define our final tables F [·], G[·]
used for decoding.

   1. Initialize with F := F ∗ , G := G∗ .

   2. If there exists an unfolded-Alice-question (E, U, L) such that

                                             Vrf F,G (E, U, L, ∗, ∗, ∗) < 2−q0 ,

      let F [E, U, L] := nil. Similarly, if there exists an unfolded-Bob-question (E ′ , U ′ , L′ ) such that

                                            Vrf F,G (∗, ∗, ∗, E ′ , U ′ , L′ ) < 2−q0 ,

      let G[E ′ , U ′ , L′ ] := nil. Repeat this process until for every unfolded-Alice-question (E, U, L)
      such that F [E, U, L] ̸= nil, we have Vrf F,G (E, U, L, ∗, ∗, ∗) ⩾ 2−q0 and for every unfolded-Bob-
      question (E ′ , U ′ , L′ ) such that G[E ′ , U ′ , L′ ] ̸= nil, we have Vrf F,G (∗, ∗, ∗, E ′ , U ′ , L′ ) ⩾ 2−q0 .

                                                           74

   It is easy to see from the definitions of F [·], G[·], the probability of agreement would decrease

possible list G of Bob, we want to show that the strategy still succeeds with probability larger than

6.6.1     Good tuples and excellent tuples
We first introduce several important definitions, starting with the notion of good tuples.
                                                                              +
Definition 6.59 (Good tuples). We say a sample (E, U, ∗, Q, E ′ , U ′ , ∗) ∼ Din is good if

   1. (E ′ , U ′ , Q) is answerable, and let (f, W ) = G [E ′ , U ′ , Q];

   2. there exists a subspace Q′ ⊆ Q with e := codimQ (Q′ ) ∈ {0, 1} such that the probability
                          h                                                                     i
                  P        F [E, U, L] = f |L Q′ ⊆ L ⊆ W ∧ Q + L ∈ MGr(E ′ , ℓ1 + e, 2ℓ1 −e · s)
             L∈MGr(E ′ ,ℓ1 ,2ℓ1 ·s)


        is at least exp(−q06 ).

    The first item in definition asserts that the pair (f, W ) is a legitimate answer for Bob, in the
sense that he is able to choose it in the strategies defined in Definition 6.58. The second item asserts
that the same function f also agrees with Alice’s table F [E, U, ·], but the guarantee is only with
respect to subspaces L from Bob’s mixed-type Grassmann domain (which is much smaller domain
compared to the domain of Alice, which is a standard Grassmann domain).16
  16
     Moreover, the agreement is defined with the same zoom-out W , a possibly smaller zoom-in Q′ ⊆ Q, and an
additional constraint of Q + L ∈ MGr(E ′ , ℓ1 + e, 2ℓ1 −e · s), but these differences are more minor.


                                                          76
    It is not very difficult to prove that a randomly chosen tuple is good with noticeable probability
(see Claim 6.61), but goodness is insufficient for our soundness analysis to go through. Indeed, it
is a priori possible that pairs (f, W ) that Bob is able to choose have noticeable agreement with
Alice’s table on Bob’s subdomain but that they cannot be extended to (f ′ , W ′ ) that agree with
Alice’s table on her own larger domain. In other words, it still may be the case that (f, W ) is
not consistent with any assignment that Alice may decode. To address this gap, we introduce the
notion of excellent tuples.
                                                                                   +
Definition 6.60 (Excellent tuples). We say a sample (E, U, ∗, Q, E ′ , U ′ , ∗) ∼ Din is excellent if

   1. (E ′ , U ′ , Q) is answerable, and let (f, W ) = G [E ′ , U ′ , Q];

   2. there exists a subspace Q′ ⊆ Q with codimQ (Q′ ) ⩽ 1 and a pair (f ′ , W ′ ) ∈ LIST⃗τQ′ (F [E, U, ·])
      such that W ⊆ W ′ and f ′ |W = f .

    The first item in the definition is the same as in Definition 6.59. The second item however
is very different, and morally asserts that (f, W ) can be extended to (f ′ , W ′ ), which is a viable
response of Alice from her list, possibly needing to shrink the zoom-in set Q to some Q′ ⊆ Q,
and/or enlarge the zoom out set to some W ′ ⊇ W . Thus, the function (f ′ , W ′ ) is a legit answer
for Alice in her strategy when she makes the correct guesses of Q′ and the codimension of W ′ , and
this is the reason we have defined Alice’s strategy in this way.
    Intuitively, it is clear that excellent tuples are very beneficial for our soundness analysis, and
indeed we show in Claim 6.63 that if the challenges of the players form an excellent tuple, then
they will make the verifier accept with noticeable probability. This is due to the fact the list of
responses of the players is bounded (after fixing choices of Q and W ), and they have at least one
pair of choices that make the verifier accept.
    Thus, it suffices to prove that a randomly chosen tuple is excellent with noticeable probability.
This turns out to be true; the precise statement is given in Claim 6.62. This is the most challenging
part of the soundness analysis, and we defer the proof to Sections 6.7 and 6.8.

The following claim shows that the probability a randomly chosen tuple is good is noticeable.
Claim 6.61. P(E,U ,∗,Q,E ′ ,U ′ ,∗)∼D+ [(E, U, Q, E ′ , U ′ ) is good ] ⩾ exp(−8ℓ21 ).

6.6.3     Completing the Soundness Analysis
Before proving the claim, we first show how to complete the soundness analysis using Claim 6.62.
Assume that in Game2c[H, k, ℓ2 , t, s1 , q0 , β], the verifier samples a tuple (E, U, A, E ′ , U ′ ) and it sends
(E, U, A) to Alice and sends (E ′ , U ′ , A) to Bob. Let Q := σ(A[q0 ] ). We prove the following claim on
the success probability of our strategy on an excellent tuple.
Claim 6.63. If (E, U, Q, E ′ , U ′ ) is excellent, the strategy wins with probability at least exp(−3Cq02 ℓ1 ).

Proof. Let (fB , WB ) = G [E ′ , U ′ , Q]. Note that by our assumption and strategies, upon getting the
tuple (E, U, Q, E ′ , U ′ ), Bob first guesses the codimension of WB , and if he makes a correct guess
(which happens with probability 1/(q0 + 1)), he randomly extends it to a linear function feB on
V(E ′ ). Alice first randomly samples a subspace Q′ ⊆ Q of codimension at most 1 and then randomly
picks a pair (fA , WA ) ∈ LIST⃗τQ′ (F [E, U, ·]) (which is non-empty since (E, U, Q, E ′ , U ′ ) is excellent),
also randomly extends it to a linear function feA on V(E). The strategy wins when all the following
events happen,

   1. WB ⊆ WA , fA |WB = fB ;

   2. feA is SNR-satisfying;

   3. feA |V(E ′ ) = feB .

Due to Lemma 3.9 and the fact that (E, U, Q, E ′ , U ′ ) is excellent, with probability at least 2−q0 ·
exp(−Cq02 ℓ1 ) ⩾ exp(−2Cq02 ℓ1 ), we have WB ⊆ WA and fA |WB = fB .
     Next, we prove that for every (fA , WA ) ∈ LIST⃗τQ′ (F [E, U, ·]), there exists a good linear function
f ′ ∈ V(E)∗ such that f ′ |WA = fA and f ′ |N (E,U ) is SNR-satisfying, which implies that conditioned on
the first event, the second event happens with probability at least 2−2q0 since codimV(E) (WA ) ⩽ 2q0 .
Let wA = codimV(E) (WA ) ⩽ 2q0 . By the definition of LIST⃗τQ′ (F [E, U, ·]), we have

6.7    Proof of Claim 6.62
In this section, we prove Claim 6.62. As discussed in Section 6.4, the proof builds on the intuition
that given a subspace Q ∈ Gr(V(E), q0 ) and an Alice-packet (E, U), if the table F [E, U, ·] has notice-
able consistency on the mixed-Grassmann domain of a lot of Bob-packets (E ′ , U ′ ) and corresponding
decodings (f, W ) (captured by the definition of good tuples), then there exists a Bob-packet (E ′ , U ′ )
and decoding (f, W ) has the ordinary Grassmann-type consistency with table F [E, U, ·] (captured
by the definition of excellent tuple). This intuition is formalized by the following lemma.

Lemma 6.64. Assume Condition 3.12. Let F [·], G[·] be the pruned tables of Alice and Bob
(see Definition 6.57). Fix an Alice-packet (E, U) and a linear subspace Q ∈ Gr(V(E), q0 ). Sup-
pose m ⩾ exp(1000 · k 5 ) is a positive integer and c1 , . . . , cm ∈ [t] are pairwise distinct indices. For
each i ∈ [m], assume (Ei′ , Ui′ ) is a ci -critical Bob-packet such that the following hold:

 (1) For every i ∈ [m], (E, U, Q, Ei′ , Ui′ ) is good; let (fi , Wi ) denote G [Ei′ , Ui′ , Q].

                                                         80
 (2) Writing Ei′ = (Ei,1 , . . . , Ei,t ) for each i ∈ [m], we have |E1,c1 | = |E2,c2 | = · · · = |Em,cm |.

Then there exist linear subspaces W ⊆ V(E) with codimV(E) (W ) ⩽ 2q0 and Q′ ⊆ Q, codimQ (Q′ ) ⩽ 1
and a linear map f : W → F2 such that
                               h                              i
                        P        F [E, U, L] = f |L Q′ ⊆ L ⊆ W ⩾ exp(−100 · q07 ).
                    L∈Gr(V(E),ℓ1 )

Furthermore, for some i ∈ [m] we have Wi ⊆ W and f |Wi = fi .

    Note that one of the conditions in this lemma requires Bob-packets to be “regular”, namely,
their critical coordinates have to be different and the size of the critical coordinate has to be the
same. Motivated by this, we introduce a useful definition.

Definition 6.65. Given an integer k ∗ ∈ {k, . . . , 3k} and a tuple (E, U, Q), we define CE,U ,Q,k∗ ⊆ [t]
to be the set of coordinates such that for every j ∈ CE,U ,Q,k∗ , there exists a j-critical Bob-packet
(E ′ , U ′ ) such           ′          ∗                      ′   ′
               S that (1) ∥E ∥min = k , (2) the tuple (E, U, E , U , Q) is good but not excellent. Define
CE,U ,Q := k∗ ∈{k,...,3k} CE,U ,Q,k∗ .

    In words, this definition captures, for a given tuple (E, U, Q), the set of possible coordinates
j ∈ [t] that support a j-critical Bob-packet (E ′ , U ′ ) such that (E, U, E ′ , U ′ , Q) is good but not
excellent. It is not hard to see that Lemma 6.64 helps to bound the size of CE,U ,Q,k∗ . We present
formal arguments below.
    The proof of Claim 6.62 then consists of two parts. The first part is to prove that for every
fixed tuple (E, U, Q) and thinning size k ∗ ∈ {k, . . . 3k}, we have

                                         |CE,U ,Q,k∗ | ⩽ exp(1000k 5 ),                                       (6.29)

using Lemma 6.64. Furthermore, we get

                                        |CE,U ,Q | ⩽ 3k · exp(1000k 5 ).

   The second part is, building upon Equation (6.29), to prove that for every Alice-packet (E, U),
we have
                                            ′   ′
                                                                                 3k · 29q0 k2 · exp(1000k 5 )
  P(E,U ,∗,Q,E ′ ,U ′ ,∗)∼D+ |(E,U ) (E, U, E , U , Q) is good but not excellent ⩽                             .
                           in                                                                    t
                                                                                                         (6.30)

Combining Claim 6.61 and a union bound, Claim 6.62 follows as a direct corollary.

Bound the size of CE,U ,Q,k∗ . We prove Equation (6.29) here. Fix an arbitrary tuple (E, U, Q)
and thinning size k ∗ ∈ {k, . . . 3k}. If the size of CE,U ,Q,k∗ is larger than exp(1000k 5 ), we are
able to find Bob-packets (E1′ , U1′ ), . . . , (Em
                                                 ′ , U ′ ), where m > exp(1000k 5 ), satisfying the condition
                                                      m
of Lemma 6.64 and any of those packets has its critical coordinate lying in the set CE,U ,Q,k∗ ; and
none of those tuples (E, U, Ei′ , Ui′ , Q) is excellent. Lemma 6.64 then shows that one of those packets
gives an excellent tuple, leading to a contradiction.




                                                       81
Bound the probability of good but not excellent tuples. It suffices to prove Equation (6.30).
                                                +
