6.8    Proof of Lemma 6.64
In this section, we prove Lemma 6.64, thereby completing the proof of Lemma 6.33.

Proof Overview. We begin with giving an overview of the argument, which is inspired by an
argument in [DKK+ 25, Min22], and for simplicity of presentation we ignore the issue of zoom-ins.
Suppose there are many Bob-packets (Ei′ , Ui′ ) and function zoom-out pairs (fi , Wi ) that are good and
their critical coordinates are all distinct. We view the Wi ⊆ V(Ei′ ) as subspaces of the larger ambient
space of Alice V(E), and note that their co-dimension becomes very large (roughly growing from q0
with respect to V(Ei′ ) to q0 + Θ(k 2 ) with respect to V(E)). However, since their critical coordinates
are distinct, we have that V(Ei′ ) + V(Ei′′ ) = V(E) for all i ̸= i′ , and thus codimV(E) (Wi + Wi′ ) ⩽ 2q0
for i ̸= i′ is constant.



                                                                82
   1. Finding a sunflower: we use Lemma 3.11 to find a collection of Wi that form a sunflower.
      By that, we mean a subspace W ⊆ V(E) that contains many of the Wi , and furthermore these
      contained subspaces satisfy that codimW (Wi ) = w and codim(Wi ∩ Wi′ ) = 2w for some fixed
      w. Informally, the subspaces Wi should be thought of as looking “random” with respect to
      the ambient space W . Our next goal is to show that Alice can find a linear function f ∈ W ∗
      that has a significant agreement with her table.

   2. Linearity testing: consider the linearity tester that picks two subspaces L1 , L2 ⊆ W of
      dimension ℓ1 that intersect in dimension ℓ1 /10 uniformly at random, and then checks that
      F [E, U, L1 ] and F [E, U, L2 ] agree on L1 ∩ L2 . It was shown in [DKK+ 25] that if F passes
      this linearity test with noticeable probability, then there exists a linear function f ∈ W ∗ with
      noticeable agreement with the table F [E, U, ·]. Thus, our goal is to show that F indeed passes
      the linearity tester with noticeable probability.

   3. Local passing: we note that since (E, U, Ei′ , Ui′ ) is good, Alice’s table has noticeable agree-
      ment with fi on subspaces from Wi . This implies that if we were to sample L1 , L2 as above
      conditioned on being contained in Wi , then with noticeable probability F [E, U, L1 ], F [E, U, L2 ]
      would both agree with fi , and thus agree between themselves.

   4. From local passing to global passing: since the number of Bob-packets is very large,
      with large probability over the choice of L1 , L2 , there will be many i such that L1 , L2 ⊆ Wi .
      Intuitively, the item above asserts that each such i gives a noticeable chance for F [E, U, L1 ],
      F [E, U, L2 ] to agree, so it should follow that F indeed passes the ℓ1 vs ℓ1 /10 linearity test
      with noticeable probability over W . We show that this is essentially correct, but for technical
      reasons we proceed somewhat differently, using the inclusion-exclusion principle on the event
      that L1 , L2 are contained in Wi and agree with fi .17

   5. Agreement with some fi : so far, we have shown that if there are many good Bob-packets,
      then Alice can decode some global linear function that has a large agreement with her table.
      To finish the proof we have to argue that she can decode a global linear function f that agrees
      with Bob’s global function fi for some i. Towards this end, we use a list-decoding argument
      to decode all f ’s for Alice that exhibit non-trivial agreement with her table, so that all of the
      passing probability of the linearity test above can be attributed to the list. We then show
      that with noticeable probability, Alice’s assignment agrees with both some function in her
      list, as well as with fi of Bob for some i, and in particular f and fi agree. A quick calculation,
      plus the fact that linear functions either fully agree or only agree on half of the space, shows
      that f and fi must fully agree for some i.

We now proceed to the formal proof.

Proof of Lemma 6.64. Since for every i ∈ [m], (E, U, Q, Ei′ , Ui′ ) is good, we know that there exists a
subspace Qi ⊆ Q with ei := codimQ (Qi ) ⩽ 1 such that

    These technical reasons do not seem very fundamental. They amount to the fact that many calculations over

W of constant codimension, become a bit trickier over the mixed-type Grassmann. Indeed, we use results such
as Proposition 6.22 and Corollary 6.23 in such situations.

   • let {y1 , . . . , yq∗ } be an arbitrary fixed basis of Q′ , let Mx := yx for every x ∈ [q ∗ ].

                                          P(L1 ,L2 )∼Pj [Zj | Xj ∧ Yj ] .

To this end, we introduce the auxiliary spaces L, T ⊆ V(Ei′j ), where T represents L1 + L2 while L
represents Q + T . We then define the following sampling procedure:

   • uniformly sample a subspace L ∈ MGr(Ei′j , D + e, 2d−e · s) conditioned on Q ⊆ L ⊆ Wij ;

   • sample a D-dimensional subspace T ⊆ L with codimL (T ) = e conditioned on T ∩ Q = Q′ ;

   • conditional on L, T , sample two subspaces L1 , L2 ∈ MGr(Ei′j , ℓ1 , 2ℓ1 · s) independently and
     uniformly among those subspaces of T containing Q′ .

By definitions, the original conditional distribution (L1 , L2 ) ∼ Pj | Xj ∧ Yj is equivalent to the
above sampling procedure if we add the condition that L1 + L2 = T . Thus, the statistical distance
between the two distributions is upper bounded by 2−Ω(ℓ1 ) (since for any D-dimensional subspaces
T , the probability uniformly sampled two subspaces Q′ ⊆ L1 , L2 ⊆ T span T 1 − 2−Ω(ℓ1 ) ).

It suffices to prove fij = f i |Wij using this probability lower bound. Assume that fij ̸= f i |Wij and
define h = fij − f i |Wij ∈ Wi∗j . Let Wi′j := ker(h) ⊆ Wij . It is easy to see that codimV(Ei′ ) (Wi′j ) =
                                                                                                                  j
w + 1. Recall that the distribution of L1 when we sample (L1 , L2 ) ∼ P | Xj ∧ Yj is defined by the
three-step procedure

    • uniformly sample a subspace L ∈ MGr(Ei′j , D + e, 2d−e · s) conditioned on Q ⊆ L ⊆ Wij ;

                                                                90
    • sample a D-dimensional subspace T ⊆ L with codimL (T ) = e conditioned on T ∩ Q = Q′ ;

    • conditioned on L, T , sample a subspace L1 ∈ MGr(Ei′j , ℓ1 , 2ℓ1 · s) independently and uniformly
      among those subspaces of T containing Q′ .
Let νj be the joint distribution of (L, T, L1 ) defined above. Next, we prove that
                                     h           i          ∗            ∗
                               P      L1 ⊆ Wi′j ⩽ 2−(D−q )+4 + 2−(ℓ1 −q ) ,

which gives a contradiction to Equation (6.43).
   We first prove the statement when Q ⊆ Wi′j . By Proposition 6.26, we have
                                         h        i         ∗

If L ⊈ Wi′j , we also have T ⊈ Wi′j and dim(Wi′j ∩ T ) = D − 1, and thus the probability that
                             ∗
L1 ⊆ Wi′j is at most 2−(ℓ1 −q ) .

             ∗
most 2−(D−q ) since the only one valid hyperplane is Wi′j ∩ L. Again, if T ⊈ Wi′j , the probability
                                   ∗
that L1 ⊆ Wi′j is at most 2−(ℓ1 −q ) .
   Combining the two cases above, we complete the proof.

A     Basic Linear Algebra
In this appendix, we collect some basic linear algebraic facts about bilinear maps. Recall from Sec-
tion 3.1 that given an F2 -vector space V and a bilinear map f : V ⊗ V → F2 , we use Tf to denote
the associated linear map from V to V ∗ . The rank of f is defined to be the rank of Tf . The first
proposition below analyzes how the rank of f changes after restricted to a subspace U ⊗U ⊆ V ⊗V .

Proposition A.1. Let V be a finite dimensional F2 -vector space and let U ⊆ V be a linear
subspace. For any bilinear form f : V ⊗ V → F2 , we have

                          rank(f ) − 2 codim U ⩽ rank(f |U ⊗U ) ⩽ rank(f ).

Proof. Let ι : U ,→ V be the embedding map, and let g = f |U ⊗U . Then we have the commutative
diagram
                                       ι        Tf         ι†
                                 U         V          V∗        U ∗.
                                                Tg


Both composing with ι on the right and composing with ι† on the left reduce the rank by at most
codim U , so we have
                        rank(Tf ) − 2 codim U ⩽ rank(Tg ) ⩽ rank(Tf ),
as desired.

                                                 95
   The next two corollaries of Proposition A.1 are both used in Lemma 4.26.

Corollary A.2. Let V be a finite dimensional F2 -vector space and let U ⊆ V be a linear subspace.
Suppose f : V ⊗ V → F2 is a bilinear form such that rank(f |U ⊗U ) = rank(f ). Then for any v ∈ V ,
there exists u ∈ U such that

                               f (v ⊗ x) = f (u ⊗ x)            for all x ∈ V.

In other words, letting g = f |U ⊗U , we have h|U ∈ image(Tg ) for each h ∈ image(Tf ).

Proof. Let ι : U ,→ V be the embedding map. Since rank(f |U ⊗U ) = rank(f ), it follows from the
proof of Proposition A.1 that
                                 image(Tf ◦ ι) = image(Tf ),
which is exactly the desired conclusion.

Corollary A.3. For any positive integers n, r and any symmetric bilinear form f : Fn2 ⊗ Fn2 → F2
with rank r, there exists an r-element subset S ⊆ [n] such that the restriction of f to FS2 ⊗ FS2 has
rank r.

Proof. Since dim ker(Tf ) = n − r, there exists an r-element subset S ⊆ [n] such that

                                             FS2 + ker(Tf ) = Fn2 .                            (A.1)

Let ι : FS2 → Fn2 be the embedding map. To show that the restriction of f to FS2 ⊗ FS2 has rank r,
it suffices to show that
                                   rank(ι† ◦ Tf ◦ ι) = rank(Tf ).
The symmetry of f implies
                                             (Tf ◦ ι)† = ι† ◦ Tf ,
so it suffices to show that rank(Tf ◦ι) = rank(Tf ). But this is immediate due to Equation (A.1).

    The next lemma is a standard decomposition of bilinear forms into symmetric rank-1 forms. It
is used also in Lemma 4.26.

Lemma A.4. Let V be a finite dimensional F2 -vector space, and let r be a positive integer. For
any symmetric bilinear form f : V ⊗V → F2 with rank r, there exist a nonnegative integer k ⩽ 3r/2
and k vectors v1 , . . . , vk ∈ V such that
                                     k
                                     X
                       f (x ⊗ y) =         f (x ⊗ vi )f (y ⊗ vi )     for all x, y ∈ V.
                                     i=1

In other words, any symmetric bilinear form of rank r has a tensor decomposition (see Defini-
tion 4.13) into at most 3r/2 linear functionals.

Proof. We proceed by induction on r. The conclusion clearly holds for r = 0. In the rest of the
proof, assume r ⩾ 1 and the conclusion is true for all smaller values of r. Consider the following
two cases.
   Case 1: there exists a vector v ∈ V such that f (v ⊗ v) = 1. Define a subspace

                                 U = {u ∈ V | f (u ⊗ v) = 0} ⊆ V.


                                                       96
It is clear that ker(Tf ) ⊆ U , and since v ∈
                                            / U we also have dim U ⩽ dim V − 1. Therefore,

          rank(f |U ⊗U ) ⩽ dim U − dim ker(Tf ) ⩽ dim V − dim ker(Tf ) − 1 = rank(f ) − 1.

Define a linear map π : V → V by letting π(x) = x − f (x ⊗ v)v for all x ∈ V . It is clear that

                     f (π(x) ⊗ v) = f (x ⊗ v) − f (x ⊗ v)2 = 0             for all x ∈ V,

and hence the image of π is contained in U . Furthermore, for each x, y ∈ V we have
                        
           f π(x) ⊗ π(y) = f (x ⊗ y) − 2f (x ⊗ v)f (y ⊗ v) + f (x ⊗ v)f (y ⊗ v)f (v ⊗ v)
                            = f (x ⊗ y) − f (x ⊗ v)f (y ⊗ v).                                        (A.2)
                                                                    
Applying the induction hypothesis to the bilinear form f π(·) ⊗ π(·) (which has rank at most
r − 1 because image(π) ⊆ U and rank(f |U ⊗U ) ⩽ r − 1), we get k vectors v1 , . . . , vk ∈ V with
k ⩽ 3(r − 1)/2 such that
                                    k
                                  X
                    f π(x) ⊗ π(y) =   f (x ⊗ vi )f (y ⊗ vi )              for all x, y ∈ V.          (A.3)
                                       i=1

Combining Equations (A.2) and (A.3), we get
                                                 k
                                                 X
             f (x ⊗ y) = f (x ⊗ v)f (y ⊗ v) +          f (x ⊗ vi )f (y ⊗ vi )    for all x, y ∈ V,
                                                 i=1

as desired (noting that k + 1 ⩽ 3r/2).
    Case 2: f (v ⊗ v) = 0 holds for all v ∈ V . Since rank(f ) ⩾ 1, there exist v, w ∈ V such that
f (v ⊗ w) = 1. It follows from the case assumption that v and w are linearly independent. Define a
subspace
                             U = {u ∈ V | f (u ⊗ v) = f (u ⊗ w) = 0} ⊆ V.
The rest of the argument is entirely analogous to Case 1. Since U ∩ span{v, w} = {0} we have
dim U ⩽ dim V − 2. Due to ker(Tf ) ⊆ U , we then have rank(f |U ⊗U ) ⩽ r − 2. Define a linear map
π : V → V by letting

                        π(x) = x − f (x ⊗ v)w − f (x ⊗ w)v              for all x ∈ V.

Similarly to Case 1, it is easy to verify that

                          f (π(x) ⊗ v) = f (π(x) ⊗ w) = 0             for all x ∈ V,

and hence the image of π is contained in U . Furthermore, for each x, y ∈ V one gets
                      
        f π(x) ⊗ π(y)
       = f (x ⊗ y) − f (x ⊗ v)f (y ⊗ w) − f (x ⊗ w)f (y ⊗ v)
       = f (x ⊗ y) − f (x ⊗ v)f (y ⊗ v) − f (x ⊗ w)f (y ⊗ w) − f (x ⊗ (v + w))f (y ⊗ (v + w)).
                                                                                      
As in Case 1, we then apply the induction hypothesis to decompose f π(x) ⊗ π(y) into the sum
of at most 3(r − 2)/2 terms of the form f (x ⊗ vi )f (y ⊗ vi ). Thus f (x ⊗ y) can be written as the
sum of at most 3(r − 2)/2 + 3 = 3r/2 such terms, as desired.

                                                       97
    The next lemma is the key to the success of the “local tensor tests” in our middle PCP, and it
is used in Lemma 5.16. In words, it asserts that if a bilinear form f has rank at least 2 + 2codimW ,
then (1) restricting it to W ⊗W drops the rank by at most 2codimW , and (2) with high probability,
restricting it to a random subspace U ⊆ W of sufficiently large dimension does not drop the rank
to at most 1.

Lemma A.5. Let V be a finite dimensional F2 -vector space, and let Q, W ⊆ V be linear subspaces
such that Q ⊆ W . Denote q = dim Q. Let ℓ be a positive integer such that q + 2 ⩽ ℓ ⩽ dim W .

                                  rank(f |U ⊗U ) ⩽ 1 Q ⊆ U ⩽ 24−(ℓ−q)/2 .

B      From Sub-4-to-1 to Exact 4-to-1 Games
In this appendix, we show that the hardness of approximating sub-4-to-1 games implies the exact
4-to-1 games theorem.
Proof of Theorem 1.6 assuming Theorem 3.3. Fix an arbitrary parameter ε ∈ (0, 1) and a positive
integer r. Write k = ⌈4/ε⌉ and r′ = kr. We will prove Theorem 1.6 by constructing a polynomial-
time reduction from GapS-4-to-1r (1, ε/2) to Gap-4-to-1r′ (1, ε).
    Given a sub-4-to-1 game instance Ψ with underlying graph (L ⊔ R, E), alphabets ΣL , ΣR and
constraints {Φe ⊆ ΣL × ΣR }e∈E , we construct a 4-to-1 game instance Ψ′ as follows:
    1. Let Ψ′ have the same constraint graph (L ⊔ R, E) as Ψ.
    2. The left-alphabet of Ψ′ is Σ′L = ΣL × (Z/kZ), and the right-alphabet of Ψ′ is Σ′R = ΣR ×
       (Z/kZ).
    3. For each edge e = (u, v) ∈ E, according to Definition 3.1, there exists a partition ΣL =
       ΣL,1 ⊔ · · · ⊔ ΣL,t into sets of size 4 and a partition ΣR = ΣR,1 ⊔ · · · ⊔ ΣR,t into singleton sets
       such that
                           [t
                     Φe =     ΓL,i × ΓR,i ,     for some subsets ΓL,i ⊆ ΣL,i and ΓR,i ⊆ ΣR,i .       (B.1)
                          i=1
       For each j, ℓ ∈ Z/kZ and i ∈ [t], we let
                     (ℓ)
                   ΣL,i,j = ΓL,i × {j} ∪ (ΣL,i \ ΓL,i ) × {j + ℓ} ⊆ ΣL × (Z/kZ) = Σ′L ,
                                                                
                                                                                                                (B.2)
                     (ℓ)
                  ΣR,i,j = ΓR,i × {j} ∪ (ΣR,i \ ΓR,i ) × {j − ℓ} ⊆ ΣR × (Z/kZ) = Σ′R .
                                                                

       We then define a constraint Φ′e,ℓ ⊆ Σ′L × Σ′R
                                                   t

       Since for each i ∈ [t], the sets ΣL,i,1 , . . . , ΣL,i,k have size |ΣL,i | = 4 and form a partition
                                                         (ℓ)              (ℓ)
       of ΣL,i × (Z/kZ), and similarly the sets ΣR,i,1 , . . . , ΣR,i,k have size |ΣR,i | = 1 and form a
       partition of ΣR,i × (Z/kZ), it is clear that Φ′e,ℓ is a 4-to-1 constraint.
                                                                                            ′
    4. For each edge e ∈ E, we put in Ψ′ the k constraints Φ′e,1 , . . . , Φe,k . The total number of
       constraints in Ψ′ is therefore equal to k|E|.
   It is easy to see that Ψ′ can be constructed from Ψ in polynomial time. We then show the
completeness and soundness of the reduction.

                                                       99
Completeness: if val(Ψ) = 1, there exist assignment maps AL : L → ΣL and AR : R → ΣR
that satisfy all constraints Φe , for e ∈ E. Then it is clear that the map A′L : L → Σ′L defined by
A′L (u) = (AL (u), 1) and the map A′R : R → Σ′R defined by A′R (v) = (AR (v), 1) jointly satisfy all
constraints Φ′e,ℓ , for e ∈ E and ℓ ∈ Z/kZ. Consequently, we have val(Ψ′ ) = 1.

Soundness: suppose we have assignment maps A′L : L → Σ′L and A′R : R → Σ′R given by
                     A′L (u) = (AL (u), KL (u))         and          A′R (v) = (AR (v), KR (v)).
For any edge e = (u, v) ∈ E, we claim that if (AL (u), AR (v)) ̸∈ Φe , then at most 2 of the k
constraints Φ′e,1 , . . . , Φ′e,k are satisfied by (A′L , A′R ). This means

                      valΨ′ (A′L , A′R ) ⩽ valΨ (AL , AR ) + 2/k ⩽ valΨ (AL , AR ) + ε/2,
and by the arbitrariness of (A′L , A′R ) it follows that
                                            val(Ψ′ ) ⩽ val(Ψ) + ε/2,
as desired.
    We now fix an edge e = (u, v) ∈ E such that (AL (u), AR (v)) ∈        / Φe , and show that at most 2 of
                   ′              ′                      ′    ′
the k constraints Φe,1 , . . . , Φe,k are satisfied by (AL , AR ). Let the subsets
                                                               (ℓ)                           (ℓ)
           ΓL,i , ΣL,i ⊆ ΣL ,      ΓR,i , ΣR,i ⊆ ΣR ,         ΣL,i,j ⊆ Σ′L ,       and      ΣR,i,j ⊆ Σ′R
be as defined in Equations (B.1) to (B.3). We divide into the following five cases.
    Case 1: (AL (u), AR (v)) ∈
                             / ΣL,i × ΣR,i for any i ∈ [t]. In this case, it is easy to see from Equa-
tions (B.2) to (B.4) that whichever values KL (u) and KR (v) take, we always have
                                                                 k

                                                             /         Φ′e,ℓ .

Therefore, none of the constraints Φ′e,1 , . . . , Φ′e,k are satisfied by (A′L , A′R ).
    Case 2: (AL (u), AR (v)) ∈ (ΣL,i \ ΓL,i ) × ΓR,i for some i ∈ [t]. In this case, (A′L (u), A′R (v)) ∈ Φ′e,ℓ
if and only if KL (u) = KR (v) + ℓ, and hence exactly one of the constraints Φ′e,1 , . . . , Φ′e,ℓ is satisfied
by (A′L , A′R ).
    Case 3: (AL (u), AR (v)) ∈ ΓL,i × (ΣR,i \ ΓR,i ) for some i ∈ [t]. This case is symmetric to the
previous case, and exactly one of the constraints Φ′e,1 , . . . , Φ′e,ℓ is satisfied by (A′L , A′R ).
     Case 4: (AL (u), AR (v)) ∈ (ΣL,i \ ΓL,i ) × (ΣR,i \ ΓR,i ) for some i ∈ [t]. In this case, we have
(AL (u), A′R (v)) ∈ Φ′e,ℓ if and only if KL (u)−ℓ = KR (v)+ℓ, and hence at most two of the constraints
   ′

Φ′e,1 , . . . , Φ′e,ℓ are satisfied by (A′L , A′R ).
   Case 5: (AL (u), AR (v)) ∈ ΓL,i × ΓR,i for some i ∈ [t]. This case cannot happen because if it
does then (AL , AR ) satisfies Φe , violating the assumption.


C     Approximate Coloring of Hypergraphs
In this appendix, we present a proof of the “starting point” theorem (restated below) used in our
reduction to 4-to-1 games. The proof of this theorem is essentially due to [DGKR05, DRS05], and
we claim no originality here. The inclusion of the proof here is purely for the sake of expositional
completeness.

                                                        100
Theorem 3.4 ([DRS05]). For every fixed positive integer c ⩾ 2, there exists a constant
ε ⩾ exp(− exp(O(c3 ))) such that given a 3-uniform hypergraph H, it is NP-hard to distinguish
between the following cases:

 (1) Yes case: H is 2-colorable.

 (2) No case: in every c-coloring of H, at least ε fraction of the edges are monochromatic.

    To prove Theorem 3.4, we need to use the PCP theorem of [ALM+ 98, AS98] and the parallel
repetition theorem of [Raz98]. More specifically, we apply the parallel repetition theorem to the
equation-vs.-variable game associated to 3SAT5 instances.19 This step is standard in the hardness
of approximation literature, and we state the result (Theorem C.2) below without proof.

Definition C.1. Let d0 and d1 be positive integers. A (d0 , d1 )-biregular projection label cover
instance Ψ = (V0 , V1 , E, Σ0 , Σ1 , (πe )e∈E ) consists of the following data:

 (1) two vertex sets V0 and V1 ;

 (2) a set E of edges between V0 and V1 such that in the bipartite graph (V0 ⊔ V1 , E), every vertex
     in V0 has degree d0 and every vertex in V1 has degree d1 ;

 (3) two finite alphabets Σ0 , Σ1 ; and

 (4) a collection of constraints (πe )e∈E , where each πe is a map from Σ0 to Σ1 .

The value of Ψ, denoted by val(Ψ), is the maximum value of
                                   1       X                                         
                                                      1 π(v0 ,v1 ) A0 (v0 ) = A1 (v1 )
                                  |E|
                                        (v0 ,v1 )∈E

achievable by assignment maps A0 : V0 → Σ0 and A1 : V1 → Σ1 .

Theorem C.2 ([ALM+ 98, AS98, Raz98]). There exists an absolute constant γ ∈ (0, 1) such that
for every fixed positive integer u, given a (3u , 5u )-biregular projection label cover instance Ψ =
(V0 , V1 , E, Σ0 , Σ1 , (πe )e∈E ) such that |Σ0 | = 7u and |Σ1 | = 2u , it is NP-hard to distinguish between
the case val(Ψ) = 1 and the case val(Ψ) ⩽ 2−γu .

       Assuming Theorem C.2, we now present a proof of Theorem 3.4.

Proof of Theorem 3.4. We proceed by a reduction from Theorem C.2. Let γ ∈ (0, 1) be as in The-
orem C.2, and let u = ⌈40γ −2 c⌉. We fix a (3u , 5u )-biregular projection label cover instance
Ψ = (V0 , V1 , E, Σ0 , Σ1 , (πe )e∈E ) with |Σ0 | = 7u and |Σ1 | = 2u ; we will show how to map it to
a 3-uniform hypergraph H while linking the value of Ψ to the colorability of H.
    The proof is divided into 8 steps. We first construct the hypergraph H in Steps 1 and 2. The
completeness of the reduction is proved in Step 3, while Steps 4 to 8 will be devoted to proving the
soundness of the reduction. Except for Step 6, the proof is almost identical to the one in [DRS05].20
Throughout the proof, we let ℓ = 2c2 .
  19
     By 3SAT5, we mean 3SAT instances where each variable is contained in exactly 5 clauses; see [Fei98, Proposition
2.1.2].
  20
     The arguments in Steps 1, 5 and 8 are due to [DGKR05].

     will be denoted by B(v) and used later in the proof.

For each i ∈ [ℓ], let c(i) ∈ [c] be a color that maximizes the number of tuples v ∈ Vi with cv = c(i) .
Since ℓ = 2c2 , by the pigeonhole principle there exists a color c∗ ∈ [c] such that c(i) = c∗ for at
least 2c distinct indices i ∈ [ℓ]. Pick exactly 2c indices among them, and let I be the set of these
2c indices.

Step 5: locating two critical layers. Observe that the bipartite graph (Vi ⊔ Vi+1 , Ei,i+1 ) is
bi-regular for all i ∈ [ℓ − 1], due to the bi-regularity of Ψ. Consider the following random walk:

   1. Start at a uniformly random element v(1) ∈ V1 .

   2. For each i ∈ [ℓ − 1], let v(i+1) be a uniformly random element of Vi+1 conditioned on
      (v(i) , v(i+1) ) ∈ Ei,i+1 .




                                                          103

random sequence (v(1) , . . . , v(ℓ) ) ∈ V1 × · · · × Vℓ that is marginally uniform over each Vi . Further-
more, by the definition of the edge sets Ei,j , it is easy to see that for any i, j ∈ [ℓ] such that i < j,
the distribution of the pair (v(i) , v(j) ) is uniform over Ei,j .

D     Grassmann Decoding Theorems
In this appendix, we explain how the Grassmann decoding theorems (Theorems 3.5 and 3.6) used
in this paper can be derived from the slightly different versions in the existing literature.

D.1    The Expansion Theorem
To prove Grassmann decoding theorems, the standard approach is to first establish some small-set
expansion properties of the Grassmann graph. It is observed in [KMS25, DKK+ 25] that small
vertex sets may not expand very well in the Grassmann graph, and the correct approach is to
consider sets that remain small even after zoom-ins and zoom-outs. This is formalized in the next
definition.

                                                       107
Definition D.1. Let n, ℓ, q be nonnegative integers such that n − q ⩾ ℓ ⩾ q + 1, and let ε ∈ (0, 1).
Suppose L ⊆ Gr(Fn2 , ℓ) is a set of subspaces. We say that L is (q, ε)-pseudorandom if for any
subspaces Q ∈ Gr(Fn2 , q) and W ∈ Gr(Fn2 , n − q) such that Q ⊆ W , we have
                                                             
                                   P n L ∈ L Q ⊆ L ⊆ W ⩽ ε.
                                        L∈Gr(F2 ,ℓ)

   To facilitate Fourier analysis, it is useful to “lift” the Grassmann graph to a Cayley graph over
an Abelian group. The “short-code graph” is a Cayley graph that serves this purpose very well,
with the matrix space Fn×ℓ
                         2   as the additive group and the rank-1 matrices as the generating set.
The counterpart of Definition D.1 in the short-code graph is given as follows.

Definition D.2. Let n, ℓ, q be nonnegative integers such that n − q ⩾ ℓ ⩾ q + 1, and let ε ∈ (0, 1).
A function f : Fn×ℓ
                2   → {0, 1} is said to be (q, ε)-pseudorandom if for any matrices

                      Y1 ∈ Fℓ×q
                            2 ,         Z1 ∈ F2n×q ,

such that X ∈ Fn×ℓ

                                                                         

                                 X∈Fn×ℓ
                                    2


      It is not hard to relate Definitions D.1 and D.2 together; we use the following lemma from [MZ26].

Lemma D.3 ([MZ26, Lemma A.18]). Let n, ℓ, q be nonnegative integers such that n−q ⩾ ℓ ⩾ q +1,
and let ε ∈ (0, 1). Suppose L ⊆ Gr(Fn2 , ℓ) is (q, ε)-pseudorandom (in the sense of Definition D.1).

    We now move on to the second decoding theorem we need. The following lemma says that if
a function F on the vertices of the Grassmann graph is locally constant on its edges, then it is
globally constant inside some zoom-in/zoom-out combination.

   Our second decoding theorem (restated below) is a similar variant of Lemma D.8 with “abundant
zoom-ins.”

E     Linearity Testing and List Decoding Bounds
The following lemma is from [KMS25, Theorem B.1] (see also [DKK+ 25, Lemma 3.1]).

Lemma E.1 ([KMS25, DKK+ 25]). There is an absolute constant C > 0 such that the following
holds. Let n, ℓ, b be positive integers such that n ⩾ 2ℓ and 40 ⩽ b ⩽ ℓ/4. Suppose F [·] is a table
where F [L] ∈ L∗ for each L ∈ Gr(Fn2 , ℓ), such that
                                          h                                i

                              (L1 ,L2 )∼G(F2 ,ℓ,b)


for some parameter ε ∈ [24−b/4 , 1]. Then there exists a linear map f : Fn2 → F2 such that
                                           h             i

                                         L∈Gr(F2 ,ℓ)

    The following lemma is a slight variant where some entries are allowed to be nil.

Lemma E.2. There is an absolute constant C ⩾ 40 such that the following holds. Let n, ℓ, b be
positive integers such that n ⩾ 2ℓ and C ⩽ b ⩽ ℓ/4. Suppose F [·] is a table where F [L] ∈ L∗ ∪ {nil}
for each L ∈ Gr(Fn2 , ℓ), such that
                                        h                                i

for some parameter ε ∈ [24−b/4 , 1]. Then there exists a linear map f : Fn2 → F2 such that
                                           h             i
                                      Pn     F [L] = f |L ⩾ C −1 ε3 .
                                         L∈Gr(F2 ,ℓ)


Proof. We define a random table F ′ [·] as follows: for each L ∈ Gr(Fn2 , ℓ),

 (1) if F [L] ̸= nil, let F ′ [L] = F [L];

 (2) if F [L] = nil, pick a uniformly random linear functional h ∈ L∗ and let F ′ [L] = h.



                                                       112
Therefore, for each L ∈ Gr(Fn2 , ℓ) the entry F ′ [L] always takes value in the space L∗ .
   For any fixed linear map f : Fn2 → F2 , the random variables

                  Zf (L) = 1 F ′ [L] = f |L and F [L] = nil         for L ∈ Gr(Fn2 , ℓ)

take values in {0, 1}, have expected values at most 2−ℓ , and are mutually independent. Therefore,
by Chernoff bound we have

In other words, there exists a realization of the random table F ′ [·], which we denote by F0 [·], such
that
 (1) for each L ∈ Gr(Fn2 , ℓ), we have F0 [L] ∈ L∗ ;

              h                                i

   The next corollary is a version of Lemma E.2 that also takes zoom-ins into account.
Corollary 6.66. There is an absolute constant C ⩾ 40 such that the following holds. Fix positive
integers n, ℓ, b and a nonnegative integer q such that n ⩾ 2ℓ and C(q + 1) ⩽ b ⩽ ℓ/4. Let Q ∈
Gr(Fn2 , q) be a subspace. Suppose F [·] is a table where F [L] ∈ L∗ ∪ {nil} for each L ∈ Gr(Fn2 , ℓ),
such that                         h                                            i
                           P n      F [L1 ]|L1 ∩L2 = F [L2 ]|L1 ∩L2 Q ⊆ L1 , L2 ⩾ ε,         (6.33)
                    (L1 ,L2 )∼G(F2 ,ℓ,b)


                                                          113
for some parameter ε ∈ [2−b/8 , 1]. Then there exists a linear map f : Fn2 → F2 such that
                                    h                    i
                             Pn       F [L] = f |L Q ⊆ L ⩾ C −1 · 2−3q ε3 .
                                    L∈Gr(F2 ,ℓ)

Proof. Since there are exactly 2q linear functionals Q → F2 , there exists a linear functional h ∈ Q∗
such that
                    h                                                              i
           P n        F [L1 ]|L1 ∩L2 = F [L2 ]|L1 ∩L2 and F [L1 ]|Q = h Q ⊆ L1 , L2 ⩾ 2−q ε,     (E.2)
      (L1 ,L2 )∼G(F2 ,ℓ,b)


    Let Qc ∈ Gr(Fn2 , n − q) be a linear subspace such that Q ∩ Qc = {0}. Define a table F ′ [·] as
follows: for each L ∈ Gr(Qc , ℓ − q),

 (1) if F [L + Q] ̸= nil and F [L + Q]|Q = h, let F ′ [L] = F [L + Q]|L ;

 (2) if F [L + Q] = nil or F [L + Q]|Q ̸= h, let F ′ [L] = nil.

It then follows from Equation (E.2) that
                                       h                                    i
                            P            F ′ [L1 ] L1 ∩L2 = F ′ [L2 ] L1 ∩L2 ⩾ 2−q ε.
                             (L1 ,L2 )∼G(Qc ,ℓ−q,b−q)

Applying Lemma E.2 to the table F ′ [·] and then translating the conclusion back to F [·] yields the
desired result.


F     Covering Properties
Several covering-property-type statements were left unproved in Sections 4 to 6. In this section,
we complete their proofs.

F.1     Covering in the Outer PCP
We first prove Proposition 4.7 (restated below), which analyzes the distribution of E ′ in a sample
(E ′ , Ain , Amid ) ∼ Jout (E, q1 , q2 , β) conditioned on (Ain , Amid ).

Proposition 4.7. Let E be a tripled set. Fix nonnegative integers q1 , q2 and a parameter β ∈ [0, 1).
Fix arbitrary tuples
                             A1 ∈ (FE2)
                                        q1
                                              and     A2 ∈ (FE   q2
                                                              2) .

Sample
                                            (E ′ , Ain , Amid ) ∼ Jout (E, q1 , q2 , β)
conditioned on the event {Ain = A1 } ∩ {Amid = A2 }. Then the resulting marginal distribution of
E ′ is independent over triples of E. Furthermore, each triple of E is entirely included in E ′ with
conditional probability at least 1 − 4q1 +q2 β.

Proof. We write q = q1 + q2 . Without loss of generality, we assume
                                                    
                E = [3k]      and     Triples(E) = {3i − 2, 3i − 1, 3i} i ∈ [k]

   Let P ⊆ E be the union of the supports of all vectors appearing in the fixed advice sequences

                                    
                                Pi = j ∈ {1, 2, 3} 3i − 3 + j ∈ P .

Consider a sample

conditioned on the event {Ain = A1 } ∩ {Amid = A2 }. Under this conditioning, the marginal
distribution of E ′ is independent over the triples of E: for every i ∈ [k],

 (1) if |Pi | ⩾ 2, then 3i − 2, 3i − 1 and 3i are all included in E ′ with probability 1;

 (2) if Pi is a singleton set {j}, then 3i − 2, 3i − 1 and 3i are all included in E ′ with probability
     1 − η1 , and with probability η1 only 3i − 3 + j is included in E ′ , where

                                  (β/3)(1 − 2−q )                      22q β

In each of the above three cases, the triple {3i − 2, 3i − 1, 3i} is included entirely in E ′ with
probability at least 1 − 22q β = 1 − 4q1 +q2 β.

F.3    Covering in the Inner PCP
We finish by stating the following general probabilistic fact that establishes the covering property
achieved by the lazy parallel repetition step. It is essentially the same as Claim 2.5, but stated
more generally in a way that is suitable for us.

Proposition F.1. Fix positive integers k, t and finite sets A1 , . . . , At with |Aj | = k for each j ∈ [t].
Suppose that for each j ∈ [t] there is a probability                      Q µj over Aj and a surjective map
                                                               Q distribution
πj : Aj → Bj onto some finite set Bj . Let π : tj=1 Aj → tj=1 Bj be the coordinate-wise map
defined by

                                                                  for all (a1 , . . . , at ) ∈ tj=1 Aj .
                                                                                              Q
              π(a1 , . . . , at ) = (π1 (a1 ), . . . , πt (at ))
                                          Qt
Define a probability distribution µ on      j=1 Aj to be the output distribution of the following process:

   1. Sample a uniformly random index c ∈ [t].

   2. Sample ac ∼ µc . For each j ∈ [t] \ {c}, sample aj uniformly at random from Aj .

  3. Output (a1 , . . . , at ).

For each b ∈ tj=1 Bj , let dist(b) be the total variation distance between
            Q

 (1) the distribution of a ∼ µ conditioned on the event {π(a) = b} (if Pa∼µ [π(a) = b] = 0, let
     dist(b) = 1), and

 (2) the uniform distribution over the subset π −1 ({b}) ⊆ tj=1 Aj .
